Title: Scoring Explanations Without Reading Them: Structural Shortcuts in Automated-Interpretability Evaluation Data

  • Authors: Xupeng Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37676
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37676
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37676]] Scoring Explanations Without Reading Them: Structural Shortcuts in Automated-Interpretability Evaluation Data(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37676)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Automated interpretability scores an explanation of a sparse-autoencoder (SAE) feature by asking a model to predict, token by token, where the feature fires on evaluation examples or to judge whether marked tokens fit it. We find that the evaluation data standard pipelines build can be scored without reading the explanation. On Neuronpedia's stored fuzzing runs for 500 random GPT-2 small features, 91.3% of activating examples are cropped with the peak on the centre token, and a rule that says "yes" when the centre token is marked reaches balanced accuracy 0.979, against 0.838 for the stored LLM scorers (recomputed from the stored per-item verdicts, on marks rebuilt from the pipeline's code); a rule reading only the mark count reaches 0.969. The cue changes form across pipelines: current delphi always marks the same slot in its fuzzing negatives, and full-context runs mark negatives with more tokens. OpenAI's simulation benchmark lists each neuron's top-activating sequences first; a rule that predicts activity on the first five scores 0.227 in pooled correlation against 0.160 for GPT-4's published simulator (post hoc). We give closed forms for what such rules can score; show that one scorer's simulation scores are higher on stored snippets than on natural windows but not shown to be reordered; and build windows that remove the position cue, derive but do not build negatives that remove the mark cues, and separate the sequence cue by decomposition. On natural-text windows cut at random offsets, the best fixed-offset prior shared by all features scores 0.015. On them, that scorer, a typed decision model (Jev), reaches a pooled correlation of 0.331 over 49 features, against 0.013 with shuffled explanations, and an open 8B simulator with the original read-out reads the explanation there (0.194 against 0.019). Scores should be reported beside the best explanation-free rule on the same items.

Title: Supervised Fine-Tuning for Reasoning Large Language Models: A Survey

  • Authors: Yongcan Yu, Kuangpu Guo, Jian Liang, Lingxiao He, Meng Wang, Ran He
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37681
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37681
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37681]] Supervised Fine-Tuning for Reasoning Large Language Models: A Survey(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37681)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large reasoning models have recently demonstrated remarkable capabilities in solving complex tasks, where supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on long chain-of-thought data serves as a crucial foundation for eliciting and enhancing their reasoning abilities. Despite rapid progress in both improving and analyzing reasoning-oriented SFT, the field still lacks a systematic survey that consolidates its fast-growing literature. To fill this gap, we present a comprehensive review of recent advancements in reasoning SFT, examining the literature through the dual lenses of methodological design and analytical investigation. First, we review methodological improvements across the SFT pipeline and categorize them into data-centric approaches and algorithm-centric innovations. Second, we reorganize analytical studies along three dimensions: data characteristics, optimization dynamics, and mechanistic insights. Finally, by synthesizing current research focuses and remaining bottlenecks, we outline promising future directions for reasoning SFT. We hope this survey deepens the understanding of reasoning SFT and paves the way for advanced reasoning models.

Title: Rank-Aware Differentially Private Release of Listwise Preferences for LLM Alignment

  • Authors: Junwei Chen, Manjiang Yu, Pengpeng Qiao, Yang Cao
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37682
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37682
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37682]] Rank-Aware Differentially Private Release of Listwise Preferences for LLM Alignment(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37682)
  • Keywords: privacy, large language model
  • Abstract: Listwise preference feedback offers richer supervision for large language model (LLMs) alignment than pairwise comparisons, but a full ranking also reveals more sensitive user preferences. Existing privacy-preserving alignment methods focus mainly on pairwise feedback, while listwise alignment under local differential privacy (LDP) remains unexplored. Extending LDP to this listwise domain raises a critical challenge: the high sensitivity of the full ranking space demands excessive noise, neutralizing the benefits of listwise supervision. To address this challenge, we analyze the interaction between supervision granularity and privacy noise in downstream training, and introduce a rank-aware exponential mechanism that privatizes listwise preference data into a low-sensitivity granularity suitable for downstream alignment. The mechanism leverages ranking information to sample a fixed-size binary partition, concentrating the noise near borderline items instead of perturbing all items uniformly as randomized response (RR) does. Empirical evaluations on downstream LLM alignment tasks show that our mechanism consistently outperforms existing LDP baselines at matched privacy budgets. The code is available at this https URL.

Title: Prompt-agnostic Language Models

  • Authors: Frederic Sadrieh
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37683
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37683
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37683]] Prompt-agnostic Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37683)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Despite their strong performance, large language models remain highly sensitive to prompt formulation. This issue is typically addressed at inference-time through manual prompt optimization, which introduces overhead and lacks scalability. Conversely, we focus on train-time approaches that build prompt robustness into the model during instruction tuning. A common strategy is to mix multiple paraphrases of each prompt into training. We demonstrate that across trainable parameters, the per-paraphrasing gradients interfere, with 57% exhibiting sign conflicts and the naive gradient average discarding approximately a fifth of the magnitude. Therefore, we develop two gradient-merging methods, Prompt-TIES and Prompt-DARE, that address this interference by leveraging interference-resolution operators from model and gradient merging and applying them to prompt gradients. In this study, we compare the efficacy of our methods to increase model robustness to train-time robustness methods spanning all three established classes: data construction, consistency regularization, and contrastive alignment. The evaluation is conducted across four models from two families at two scales. We observe the interaction between prompt phrasing and the specific examples accounts for 54% of per-example variability on average, and exceeds 70% on tasks such as ANLI and WiC. The gradient-merging methods directly improve this local robustness and nearly halve per-example variance on fully fine-tuned models at little cost to overall accuracy. The other train-time methods enhance raw performance, but fail to improve per-example variance reliably. Code: this https URL. Model checkpoints: this https URL.

Title: An Introduction to Large Language Models for Scientists and Professionals

  • Authors: Umberto Michelucci
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37684
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37684
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37684]] An Introduction to Large Language Models for Scientists and Professionals(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37684)
  • Keywords: security, privacy, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly become important tools for science, education, medicine, and professional work. Despite their apparent simplicity from the user's perspective, effective and responsible use requires an understanding of how these systems work, what their limitations are, and how their outputs should be evaluated. I wrote this document as a basis for my lecture on LLM in the master in artificial intelligence in medicine at the university of Turin, Italy. This book provides a practical and accessible introduction to LLMs for scientists and professionals. It explains the fundamental ideas behind tokenisation, embeddings, training, inference, context windows, prompting, and instruction hierarchies, while deliberately avoiding mathematical detail. Particular attention is given to the limitations of LLM-generated answers, including hallucinations, uncertainty, reproducibility, bias, and the need for systematic verification and human oversight. The book also discusses privacy, governance, security, and the responsible handling of personal and sensitive information. More advanced topics include Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), retrieval quality, chunking, embeddings, vector search, agents, tools, and workflows. A substantial practical exercise guides the reader through running a local LLM with Ollama and Open WebUI and using it to interact with a collection of scientific papers. The exercise emphasizes evidence retrieval, source verification, uncertainty, disagreement between studies, and the distinction between plausible language and scientifically supported conclusions. No programming knowledge is required.

Title: From Geometry to Behavior: How Instruction Exposure Unlocks Latent Rhetorical Directions in LLMs

  • Authors: Shengjie Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37685
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37685
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37685]] From Geometry to Behavior: How Instruction Exposure Unlocks Latent Rhetorical Directions in LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37685)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Language models do not merely memorize facts; they absorb rhetorical strategies from pretraining data, including the human tendency to reason by analogy. We ask: is this absorbed capability geometrically accessible before any instruction tuning, and what determines whether it can be activated at inference time? We extract an "Analogy Vector" from the residual stream via difference-in-means and show it enables steerable explanation style across seven instruction-tuned models (Gemma-2, Llama-3.1, Qwen-2.5; 2B-32B), improving harmonic mean from 3.22 to 3.94 (+22%) for Gemma-2-9B-IT on 300 held-out test prompts. A small human validation study confirms the LLM judge on Analogy Quality. Our central finding, from seven matched base-instruct pairs, is a gradient of behavioral accessibility: the analogy direction exists in every base model, but its behavioral accessibility under linear intervention scales continuously with instruction exposure during training. Gemma-2 base models (strict pretraining/IT separation) show flat steering landscapes: present but behaviorally inert. Qwen-2.5 base models (instruction data woven into pretraining) are already partially accessible before any explicit fine-tuning; base-instruct cosine similarity of the analogy vector reaches 0.94-0.97 for Qwen versus 0.37-0.66 for Gemma, providing geometric corroboration of the behavioral gradient. This challenges the binary "base vs instruct" framing: the relevant variable is how much instruction exposure a model has received across its full training pipeline. We position activation steering as a complementary interpretability tool that reveals how instruction exposure organizes rhetorical knowledge in activation space.

Title: Bouba Beats Kiki, and Other Regularities in the Subjective Preferences of LLMs

  • Authors: Katy Blumer, Jon Kleinberg, Ravi Kumar, Andrew Tomkins
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37692
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37692
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37692]] Bouba Beats Kiki, and Other Regularities in the Subjective Preferences of LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37692)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Users engage with large language models on tasks that are purely objective ("When was Netflix founded?"), purely subjective ("What should I name my child?") or anywhere in-between. We have rich tools for evaluating model accuracy with respect to objective criteria. However, when it comes to subjective aspects of a task, we do not have similarly developed methodology at hand. In this paper, we take some early steps towards framing the question of subjective evaluation. Rather than measure how often a model is right or wrong on a question, we instead ask, how do models make choices among multiple viable responses without a clear winner? To study this question, we restrict our focus to short-form content, and consider comparison tasks among three such content types: numbers, baby names, and fictional cities. We identify 29 aspects of these short-form examples covering many areas of syntax and semantics (length, alphabetic order, popularity, vowel mix, spoken sound, etc.). We show first that models tend to have strong and reproducible preferences for or against some of these aspects. Second, we show that variations in how the model is asked to express a preference may cause some of these aspects to activate or deactivate in the model's selection. Third, we show that overall preference distributions among pairs may vary radically based on seemingly-unimportant variations in the nature of the question posed to the model, raising new questions about manipulation. And finally, we show that, if models are used both to create lists of alternatives and to rank them, there are unexpected interactions between the two stages that may also drive the models' stated preferences.

Title: Threshold Compatibility and Support-Selection Diagnostics in Neural Discovery of Partial Differential Equations

  • Authors: Zeen Song
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37726
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37726
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37726]] Threshold Compatibility and Support-Selection Diagnostics in Neural Discovery of Partial Differential Equations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37726)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Accurate regression need not yield the correct equation after support selection. For a median/sample-standard-deviation threshold in neural PDE discovery, we derive an exact two-signal retention boundary and a complete-vector perturbation bound. Exact advection-diffusion libraries lose diffusion despite coefficient recovery to numerical precision. True-support refits of forty saved sparse states reduce diffusion error without restoring all derivative accuracy. A fixed sequential threshold ridge comparator recovers exact support in sixty ideal controls but selects extra terms in every learned state. All-fold correct-support candidates are rejected by conditional validation; post hoc energy pooling preserves their higher minimum loss in all sixty states. Supplied-support estimates outperform unchanged selected equations in all 240 physical-residual comparisons on four unused analytic trajectories. With coefficients, observation points and weights fixed, exact replacement of the learned library and target reverses their learned-row ordering in 59/60 states. Weighting both losses also fails the specified Burgers diffusion-benefit criterion at every tested budget. These finite diagnostics distinguish threshold incompatibility, refit damage and score rejection; they establish neither automatic correction nor general recovery or predictive stability.

Title: Predicate Neuron: Differentiable Quantization of Predicate Relations in Gradient-based Learning

  • Authors: Ioannis Konstantoulas, Iliana Loi, Kyriakos Sgarbas
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37727
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37727
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37727]] Predicate Neuron: Differentiable Quantization of Predicate Relations in Gradient-based Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37727)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: In machine learning, symbolic reasoning approaches often rely on static architectures that hinder integration into deep neural networks and gradient-based optimization. To address this, we introduce the Native Predicate Neuron, a differentiable logic-reasoning neural module that enriches standard architectures with symbolic reasoning capabilities via first-order logic quantization. The proposed neuron models relational logic by learning to alternate between operations using a single trainable parameter. It features a tailored gradient mechanism with a dual-nature formulation: executing hard logical operations during training while maintaining differentiability for backpropagation. By quantizing both its input and choice state, the Predicate Neuron approximates continuous and non-continuous functions, effectively resolving discontinuities that standard deep learning architectures struggle with. We evaluate the Predicate Neuron across Fully-Connected, Convolutional, Recurrent, and Transformer architectures on benchmarks like MNIST and Wikitext. Results show that incorporating Predicate Neurons accelerates convergence, boosts accuracy, and lowers loss in general, with particularly strong improvements in tasks requiring monotonic reasoning or handling discontinuities. Overall, the Predicate Neuron natively integrates into standard neural networks to enable fuzzy logic operations and is available in Keras and PyTorch.

Title: Selective Transfer Learning for Groundwater Level Anomaly Forecasting: Accuracy-Risk Trade-offs

  • Authors: Yaxuan Wu, Ali Mohammed Mansoor Alsahag, Seyed Sahand Mohammadi Ziabari
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37733
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37733
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37733]] Selective Transfer Learning for Groundwater Level Anomaly Forecasting: Accuracy-Risk Trade-offs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37733)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: This work studies context-aware selective transfer learning for multi-well groundwater anomaly forecasting in the Netherlands. Source-domain pretraining can raise average accuracy but is not uniformly reliable: it degrades performance on a substantial fraction of heterogeneous wells. Context-Aware Selective Learning (CASL) uses calibration-trained, constrained contextual blending of frozen no-transfer and fixed-transfer parents. Global Convex Blend uses the same parents with one calibration-fitted weight per seed. The corrected task requires nine consecutive observed monthly inputs and an exact three-calendar-month target, without interpolation. Evaluation covers 457 wells and 13,718 test windows under five predefined seeds, with the four-cell maxsim0.60 source pool frozen using Always-TL validation RMSE alone. Fixed transfer harms 18.38-51.86% of wells. CASL has 0.424-1.084% lower RMSE than No TL, partially rescuing 97.53-99.50% and fully rescuing 23.46-43.78% of fixed-harmed wells. Partial rescue can arise structurally from convex blending and does not establish contextual superiority. Global Convex Blend protects more wells in four seeds, while CASL has slightly lower pooled RMSE in four. Neither dominates: the results support an accuracy-risk trade-off on the fixed split, not rolling deployment validity.

Title: When Skills Tell Agents Not to Ask: A Large-Scale Study of Oversight Directives in Agent Skills

  • Authors: Timofey Sanko, Yuan Tian
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37734
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37734
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37734]] When Skills Tell Agents Not to Ask: A Large-Scale Study of Oversight Directives in Agent Skills(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37734)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Agent skills, reusable instruction packages centred on a this http URL file, extend AI agents on demand, and a single line in a widely copied skill reaches every agent that loads it. Some lines are oversight-loosening directives: they tell the agent to proceed without approval, to bypass checks with --no-verify, or to keep working without checking in. Although such directives can remove a checkpoint from every agent that loads them, no prior work has examined them. We present the first large-scale study of oversight-loosening directives, analyzing 1.53 million English skills from GitSkills. We split skills into units (sentences, bullets, table cells, and code lines), develop a codebook that labels oversight directives by polarity, mechanism, and scope, label 2,097 units by hand, and train a detector to scale the analysis. Our results show that about one in seventeen skills (about 88,000 skills) contains a loosening directive. These directives most often loosen oversight through permission flags, and most of them (70.1%) apply unconditionally rather than only in specific contexts such as CI. Compared with skills that have the same amount of oversight language, skills that run shell code, install packages, or use sudo have 1.7-1.8 times the odds of containing a loosening directive, an association that holds across all our robustness checks, and they do not compensate with more tightening. Loosening spreads through a few upstream skills rather than through edits by their adopters: 2.4% of the skill families that carry loosening account for half of all copies of loosening-carrying skills, and 1.1% of distinct loosening units for half of all loosening occurrences. Open-source skill scanners catch at most 46% of loosening directives, with a precision below 0.4, while the LLM behind them, prompted with our codebook instead, catches 61% at a precision of 0.71.

Title: Poisoned Skills: Propagating Jailbreaks Through Agent Markdown Files

  • Authors: Kashyap R Manjusha, Murali Emani
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37738
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37738
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37738]] Poisoned Skills: Propagating Jailbreaks Through Agent Markdown Files(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37738)
  • Keywords: attack, steal
  • Abstract: Modern agent frameworks allow users to provide Markdown files containing system prompts, tool descriptions, and skill definitions (e.g., this http URL), which are incorporated into the agent's context during initialization. In this work, we investigate whether this configuration mechanism can also serve as an injection channel: can a jailbreak embedded in a Markdown skill file propagate through an agent and induce prohibited behavior? To answer this question, we construct a LangChain-based question-answering agent whose system prompt is assembled from a user-provided Markdown file. We embed a jailbreak combining persona manipulation with a GPTFUZZER-based attack and evaluate its effectiveness across seven contemporary open-weight models using 270 forbidden questions spanning nine harm categories. Under a benign system prompt, the models exhibit near-complete refusal behavior, with a mean attack success rate (ASR) of just 0.5%. In contrast, the injected skill file raises the mean ASR to 45%, reaching 68.9% for individual models. We further evaluate two payload obfuscation strategies: an invisible zero-width character-based stealth payload and a phonetic payload based on grapheme-to-phoneme transformations. Neither outperforms the plain-text injection; notably, the phonetic variant reduces mean ASR to 30%. These findings identify Markdown skill files as an underexplored attack surface for AI agents and suggest that vulnerability to configuration-based jailbreaks depends more strongly on the underlying model than on the specific payload obfuscation technique.

Title: Agentic Backdoors via Pretraining Poisoning

  • Authors: Xinyan Hu, Pingbang Hu, Chloe Loughridge, Xuanli He, Nicholas Carlini, Matthew Jagielski, Javier Rando, Yiming Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37741
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37741
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37741]] Agentic Backdoors via Pretraining Poisoning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37741)
  • Keywords: security, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are pretrained on vast, uncurated corpora scraped from the public web. This creates a security risk from attackers who plant poisoned content in the pretraining data. We show for the first time that such poisoning can plant a remote code execution backdoor in an agent, causing it to execute attacker-chosen commands on the user's machine. To measure the threat end to end, we pretrain Qwen3 models from scratch at sizes 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B, and then post-train each into a CLI agent using a clean pipeline. Poisoning only 0.1% of the pretraining corpus with our new poison design implants the backdoor: the deployed agent executes the malicious command when the trigger is present while behaving normally on clean inputs. The attack success rate (ASR) increases with model scale, with the 4B model reaching roughly 92% ASR at a 0.1% poison rate and staying positive even when the poisoning rate drops to 0.0005%. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Multi-Draft On-Policy Distillation for Diffusion Drafters

  • Authors: Petr Molodyk, Yuchen Zhu, Jaemoo Choi, Molei Tao, Yongxin Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37778
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37778
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37778]] Multi-Draft On-Policy Distillation for Diffusion Drafters(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37778)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Speculative decoding is used to reduce the inference cost of language models. One-step diffusion drafters propose a block of multiple tokens in parallel, reducing the sequential cost of speculative decoding. On-policy distillation trains these drafters using verifier feedback on their own proposals. However, parallel drafting makes this feedback difficult to use: the drafter predicts each token without observing the preceding tokens sampled within the same block, while the verifier conditions its prediction on those tokens. Different sampled prefixes therefore give different verifier targets for the same drafter prediction. We show that distilling against these targets separately can suppress tokens that are plausible after some prefixes but unlikely after others, harming draft acceptance. We explain the effects of this mismatch and introduce Multi-Draft On-Policy Distillation (MDraft), a new parallel drafter distillation method which samples several draft blocks from the same context and aggregates the verifier's full-vocabulary conditionals across their prefixes. We use all the drafts to collect information from the verifier, allowing support from alternative prefixes to influence the training target. The resulting algorithm improves the acceptance length of a parallel drafter beyond the level of pre-training, and alleviates the effects of the distributional mismatch. MDraft changes only training, retaining the drafter's architecture and inference procedure, allowing the resulting drafter to be used as-is in place of the pretrained checkpoint. Experiments on a suite of mathematical, coding, and conversational tasks show longer accepted drafts than prior distillation methods.

Title: Sparse tensor networks for individual tree diameter estimation in mixed forests

  • Authors: Clara Lößl, Fabian Ewald Fassnacht, Viacheslav Barkov
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37784
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37784
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37784]] Sparse tensor networks for individual tree diameter estimation in mixed forests(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37784)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Measurements of tree diameter at breast height (DBH) are fundamental to forest inventories, but manual surveys across entire stands or regions are impractical. UAV-borne laser scanning (ULS) enables scalable automated forest surveys, yet canopy occlusion makes accurate DBH estimation from ULS point clouds non-trivial. Limited stem-level point density of ULS data constrains approaches relying on detailed stem reconstruction, such as Quantitative Structure Models (QSMs) and circle fitting. Empirical machine learning methods based on predefined point cloud metrics are an alternative. New deep learning approaches offer an even more principled solution, allowing models to learn directly from point clouds. Sparse tensor networks have shown particular promise, transforming forestry tasks such as tree species classification. We investigate whether these strengths can be extended to estimate individual tree DBH from ULS data across four mixed-species temperate forest plots in Germany. We further advance existing sparse tensor networks by introducing TreeNext, a lightweight deep learning architecture for direct individual tree parameter estimation from voxelized LiDAR point clouds. We present a fully automated forest inventory pipeline from individual tree instance segmentation to DBH regression, and quantify the impact of segmentation errors by comparing performance on automatically segmented and ground truth instances. TreeNext achieves an $R^2$ of 0.8355 on automatically segmented trees and 0.9380 on ground truth segments, outperforming strong tabular baselines including Random Forest and XGBoost. Segmentation quality emerges as the dominant error source in the automated pipeline. TreeNext proves highly data-efficient, performing well even in small data scenarios. Our findings establish sparse tensor networks as a practical foundation for automated individual tree DBH estimation from ULS.

Title: Spokes, not a Ring: Revisiting the Weekday Manifold in Language Models

  • Authors: Diksha Gupta, Rieke Fruengel, Stefan Heimersheim
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37796
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37796
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37796]] Spokes, not a Ring: Revisiting the Weekday Manifold in Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37796)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Recent work has hypothesised that large language models may represent some concepts as multi-dimensional manifolds instead of single directions. Using these manifolds to interpret or steer representations often assumes that moving along them corresponds to a continuous change in the represented concept. In this work, we test this continuous manifold assumption on the prototypical weekday manifold. Despite its striking circular geometry, we find that the weekday representations behave more like seven discrete states arranged in a circle than a continuous ring. First, we show that local sensitivity has seven minima aligned with the weekdays, producing a step-like mapping between representation and behaviour under on-manifold steering. Second, we test whether any concepts are represented along the manifold outside the seven discrete states, and find neither time of day, uncertainty, nor natural text populate the intermediate space. Together, these results challenge the continuous interpretation of the weekday manifold and motivate testing whether movement along other representation manifolds correspond to meaningful changes in model representations and behaviour.

Title: Structured Security Explanations for Learning-Based Vulnerability Detection

  • Authors: Fares Trad, Yizhou Chen, Hung Viet Pham, Zeyu Sun, Gias Uddin
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37797
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37797
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37797]] Structured Security Explanations for Learning-Based Vulnerability Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37797)
  • Keywords: security, large language model
  • Abstract: Software vulnerability detection remains difficult because existing approaches rely on predefined analysis rules, learn vulnerability patterns implicitly from code and labels, or use large language models (LLMs) to make the final security judgment. It remains unclear whether structured descriptions of security-relevant program behavior can improve supervised detection. We present VulnLens, an explanation-augmented framework for binary C/C++ vulnerability detection that separates program-behavior analysis from the final decision. A label-blind LLM-based Explainer examines each function from seven fixed perspectives: data flow, memory access, object lifetime, arithmetic and type behavior, control flow, shared state, and external-resource access. Without receiving vulnerability labels or CWE information, it generates structured explanations. A supervised Detector combines source code and explanations through sequential cross-attention. We evaluate VulnLens on a PrimeVul-derived dataset covering ten CWE categories and test transfer to SecVulEval without retraining. On the 526-function PrimeVul test set, VulnLens achieves 75.5% accuracy and 77.2% F1, exceeding DeepSeek V4 Pro by 4.2 percentage points in accuracy and Gemini 2.5 Pro by 2.2 points in F1. Matched explanations improve F1 by 7.3 points over a separately trained code-only detector. On SecVulEval, VulnLens achieves 66.4% accuracy and 65.8% F1, outperforming DeepSeek V4 Pro by 7.6 points in accuracy and 20.0 points in F1, and Gemini 2.5 Pro by 16.6 points in accuracy and 7.8 points in F1.

Title: GOFish: Full Field-of-View Gaussian Splatting and Mesh Extraction

  • Authors: Allen Tu, Meher Gitika Karumuri, Shifan Zhu, Hailing Wang, Yen-Liang Lin, David Held, Matthias Zwicker, Tom Goldstein
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.GR, cs.RO

Title: Responsible Quantum Machine Learning for Medical Imaging: A Fairness Audit Across Sensitive Subgroups

  • Authors: Giovanni dos Santos Franco, Felipe Mahlow, Ellison Fernando Cardoso, José Adenaldo Santos Bittencourt, Felipe Fanchini
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37819
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37819
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37819]] Responsible Quantum Machine Learning for Medical Imaging: A Fairness Audit Across Sensitive Subgroups(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37819)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Quantum machine learning (QML) is increasingly explored for medical classification, yet its behaviour under subgroup-aware fairness criteria remains understudied. Two scenarios are evaluated: dermatological lesions on DDI (sensitive attribute: Fitzpatrick skin type) and chest X-rays on CheXpert-derived data (sensitive attribute: age group). Fairness effects are model- and dataset-dependent, so QML requires empirical subgroup-aware evaluation. All quantum models run in classical simulation; we claim no quantum advantage.

Title: Learning Without Clocks: Partition-Invariant Neural Network

  • Authors: Han Wang, Jingyuan Bao, Juntao Wu, Saiao Zhou, Yuman Nie
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37823
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37823
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37823]] Learning Without Clocks: Partition-Invariant Neural Network(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37823)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Event cameras, spiking cochleas and similar sensors emit asynchronous events, yet most learning systems first bin them into frames or packets, so the output depends on how the stream was sliced. Networks whose state is updated exactly at every event and evolves in closed form between events are invariant to this slicing. We ask what more makes the output (i) independent of the processing order of simultaneous events and (ii) continuous in the time stamps, even when two events cross in time, while (iii) staying sensitive to temporal order. For arbitrary nonlinear event models we prove that (i) and (ii) are each equivalent to pairwise commutation of the event updates, and that once updates commute, all order dependence comes from their non-commutation with the inter-event dynamics and vanishes linearly in the time lag. This identifies a class of time-mediated non-commutative (TM) models and yields a resolution-robustness bound for any model. We construct the minimal TM layer, a learnable, provably Lipschitz generalization of the Hassenstein-Reichardt correlator; prove that no single additive layer can uniformly represent ordered pair interactions, whereas a three-dimensional TM state can; show that each TM layer adds two levels of temporal nesting versus one for additive layers; and give a single-layer higher-order construction (TM-k) and a stacking rule preserving all three properties. We instantiate these results as the Clock-Free Event (CFE) layer, linear in the number of events, give a topology-free variant for temporal point processes with closed-form likelihoods, and specify a preregistered clock-robustness protocol with a physical-time threat model for spike-retiming attacks. In preliminary development-set experiments, the TM state beats an otherwise identical additive state by 3.5-9.1 points on DVS128 Gesture and by 24.5 and 30.6 points on SHD and Speech Commands.

Title: DeFT-RAG: Incrementally Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers

  • Authors: Dingyi Kang, Bingzhe Li
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37826
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37826]] DeFT-RAG: Incrementally Decomposing Multi-Hop Questions into Self-Contained Sub-Questions with Reusable Answers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37826)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets a large language model (LLM) answer questions by retrieving relevant passages from a large corpus. However, RAG struggles with multi-hop questions, which require chaining facts spread across several documents through intermediate entities that the question never mentions. Graph-based RAG methods retrieve once from the entities a question names and so rarely recover the full evidence chain. Iterative RAG methods recover it step by step, but their accuracy is limited by the many tasks they ask the LLM to perform, such as planning the sub-questions, deciding their dependencies, rewriting them as answers arrive, and keeping track of what is known. We propose DeFT-RAG, a RAG framework built on three components. First, DeFT-RAG generates one self-contained sub-question at a time from the original question and the sub-questions answered so far with their answers (QA pairs). Each sub-question names every entity it asks about, which removes the need to plan, track dependencies or rewrite, and lets the LLM focus on a single fine-grained task at a time. Second, DeFT-RAG moves the remaining LLM work outside the LLM: rule-based updates to a QA table that keeps track of what is known, and yes/no decisions made by a fine-tuned lightweight decision model. Third, DeFT-RAG caches the QA pairs and reuses an answer when a later question asks a sub-question with the same meaning. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, DeFT-RAG achieves the best accuracy among thirteen baselines with both gpt-4o-mini and Qwen3.6 as the backbone, while giving the LLM fewer input tokens than any other iterative method. With a QA cache shared across 1,000 MuSiQue questions, 23.1% of sub-questions are answered from the cache, which reduces LLM calls by 12.9% without lowering accuracy.

Title: Improving Data-Constrained Scaling with Hyperparameter Transfer and Ensembling

  • Authors: Ethan Baron, Shmuel Berman, Anirudh Satheesh, Akshay Vegesna, Samip Dahal, Andrew Gordon Wilson
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37842
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37842
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37842]] Improving Data-Constrained Scaling with Hyperparameter Transfer and Ensembling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37842)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As growing compute budgets outpace the growth of high-quality training data, large language models must be trained with both compute and data constraints in mind. Unfortunately, existing prescriptions for data-constrained scaling suffer from two important limitations. First, past works hold key hyperparameters such as weight decay fixed with scale, despite the fact that optimal hyperparameters vary wildly with model size and number of epochs. Second, past works neglect ensembling, a promising axis for scaling under data constraints. As a surprising consequence, prior prescriptions can lead to loss that increases with compute budget. We address both limitations by fitting power laws that prescribe how to transfer hyperparameters with scale and scaling laws that prescribe how to allocate compute with ensembling. Training on 100M tokens from FineWeb, our recipe reduces validation loss by over 0.15 nats compared to prior recipes, a 14% decrease in perplexity, and achieves monotonic improvements with compute through ensembling. Title: Set an Exploit to Catch an Exploit: Compiling Public Proofs of Concept into Threat Hunting Rules

  • Authors: Muhammad Shoaib, Alexander Suh, Joshua Andrew Tomiak, Wajih Ul Hassan
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37872
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37872
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37872]] Set an Exploit to Catch an Exploit: Compiling Public Proofs of Concept into Threat Hunting Rules(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37872)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Public exploit code for a newly disclosed vulnerability usually appears before any published rule that detects its exploitation. Of the 45 CVEs we evaluate, 37 ever received a Sigma, Elastic or Splunk rule; in 33 the exploit came first, by a median of 22 days. Analysts still write every rule by hand. We present Encore, which compiles a public Python proof-of-concept exploit (PoC) into CVE-specific hunting rules over a web service's existing logs, without running it. Encore rests on the PoC Behavior Alignment Principle: a PoC performs the exploit, so its code already provides a rule's HTTP requests, payloads and host operations. That is hard to act on: a PoC is written against libraries and runs on the attacker's machine, while the target's web-server, proxy and host logs record only what arrived and what it did. Encore closes the gap with a map from library calls to the operations they cause, built once per library, keeping only the operations visible on the target. For 12 of the 45, Encore's rule states the exploit's own payload fragment or host operation, which is evidence of the exploit itself, and not of traffic to its endpoint. On matched controls it separates that exploit from the same request with a harmless value, and it raises no alerts across 27,081 legitimate and crawl requests. We never compile the exploit that produced the logs. Most are Splunk's public attack data, recorded by a third party. Without reading a single log, Encore's rules find 43 of the 45. Splunk's log-tested rules cover 30 of the 45, and Encore finds 28 of those, plus all 10 CVEs without an ESCU rule. Where Splunk's rules alert on 20 wrong CVE pairs, Encore's alert on only 2. Hunting can start as soon as a PoC is public, and rules can raise an alert when it provides a payload or host operation.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37881

  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Deepfake detection models achieve high overall accuracy but exhibit large performance disparities across demographic groups, causing some groups to experience substantially higher false-positive rates than others. Existing fairness-aware methods address this by constraining model outputs to equalize group predictions, but do not tackle the underlying feature-level bias. As a result, models may still rely on demographic characteristics, such as skin texture, as indirect cues for detecting manipulated content. To address this problem, we propose Intersectional Invariant Learning (IIL), a framework that improves feature learning rather than only adjusting model outputs. IIL alternates between identifying the most challenging demographic partitions and learning features that remain consistent across those partitions. The framework also includes a false-positive-rate fairness objective and group-balanced sampling across six gender x skin-tone intersectional groups. We evaluate IIL on a large-scale fairness benchmark containing over one million images, 10 baseline detectors, and 21 fairness and utility metrics. Results show that IIL achieves Pareto-optimal performance, simultaneously attaining an AUC above 99%, a false-positive rate under 2.5%, and the lowest intersectional fairness disparity (FMEO) among all compared methods. IIL outperforms prior fairness-aware methods on the large majority of the 21 evaluation metrics, while reducing both false-positive rates and intersectional fairness disparities by several-fold compared with the strongest competing method.

Title: FYC: FPR-Disparity Weight Perturbation and Youden Calibration for Fair Deepfake Detection

  • Authors: Uzoamaka Ezeakunne, Taiwo Olabamipe, Deevesh Jain, Xiuwen Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37882
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37882
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37882]] FYC: FPR-Disparity Weight Perturbation and Youden Calibration for Fair Deepfake Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37882)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Deepfake detectors routinely achieve high aggregate accuracy while exhibiting substantial demographic disparities in their errors: false-positive rates differ by double digits across gender and skin tone. Most existing remedies require either full retraining with a fairness-aware objective or demographic-annotated training data, both impractical when only a pretrained checkpoint is available, while label-free post-hoc calibrators leave the detector's weights unchanged. We introduce FYC (FPR-disparity Weight Perturbation and Youden Calibration), a two-stage post-hoc method that perturbs a detector's weights under an FPR-disparity criterion, then calibrates a separate decision threshold for each demographic group - requiring no architectural changes and no full retraining. Evaluated against every detector benchmarked on AI-Face v2 across six intersectional gender x skin-tone groups, FYC outperforms the field on 18 of 21 reported fairness and utility metrics, including purpose-built fairness architectures, at a fraction of the training cost. We further identify why the choice of fairness criterion is critical: the original Demographic Parity objective used in prior weight-perturbation work is architecture-dependent and unpredictable, improving two of four evaluated architectures, leaving a third largely unchanged, and substantially degrading the fourth (PG-FDD), with no way to anticipate the outcome for a given checkpoint in advance. Conditioning the same objective on real images only - our FPR-disparity criterion - eliminates this unpredictability, improving fairness and accuracy jointly across all four architectures with no exceptions.

Title: SAC-GCL: Structure-Aware Compression for Graph Contrastive Learning

  • Authors: Muhammad Ifte khairul Islam, Tanvir Hossain, Esra Akbas
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.SI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37898
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37898

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Graph Contrastive Learning (GCL) has achieved remarkable success in self-supervised representation learning by maximizing agreement between augmented views of a graph. Although augmented view generation is the central step in GCL, existing models rely on stochastic augmentation of the input graph, such as edge perturbation and node masking, which lack structural awareness and frequently destroy critical topology or introduce semantically inconsistent views. While recent adaptive methods improve upon random perturbations, they primarily operate on localized node or edge neighborhoods and fail to capture higher-order, macro-level structural organization crucial for graph classification. To address this limitation, we propose Structure-aware Compression for Graph Contrastive Learning (SAC-GCL), a novel framework that employs structural decomposition and graph compression as a principled view generation strategy. Moving beyond random augmentations, SAC-GCL leverages structural decomposition techniques to extract cohesive subgraphs and condense them into supernodes, constructing coarsened graph views that preserve local density and capture higher-level structural patterns while reducing noise and redundancy. We leverage multiple cohesive subgraph models to derive complementary views of the graph, each capturing distinct structural properties. To capture multi-resolution information, a hierarchical view encoder processes these compressed views by initializing super-node features with shared-weight cohesive subgraph embeddings. Our results from extensive experiments on six benchmark datasets demonstrate that structure-aware compression provides a powerful alternative to random perturbations, yielding more robust and discriminative graph representations for graph classification tasks.

Title: Muon Makes the First Mixture-of-Experts Layer Harder to Balance

  • Authors: Marcos Hernanz
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37914
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37914

  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Many open mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models train with Muon instead of AdamW. Most balance the load with a bias on each expert's routing score, adjusted after every step. The span grows when all tokens favor the same experts, so it shows how hard a layer is to balance. On the smaller model, halving Muon's learning rate removed most of the rise and slightly improved the loss. Moving only the router to AdamW made the loads shift more during the warmup and slightly raised the loss, so at this scale the results favor keeping the router on Muon.

Title: FAD-KV: Adaptive Dictionary KV Caching for Efficient Reasoning Decoding

  • Authors: Yuxuan Zhu, David H. Yang, Parisa Shiravani, Mohammad Mohammadi Amiri, Tejaswini Pedapati, Subhajit Chaudhury, Keerthiram Murugesan, Pin-Yu Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37921
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37921
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.37921]] FAD-KV: Adaptive Dictionary KV Caching for Efficient Reasoning Decoding(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37921)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Key-value (KV) caching is a core mechanism in large language models, where intermediate key and value representations are stored to accelerate autoregressive decoding. As generation length increases, KV cache memory and computation grow linearly, making compression critical for efficient long-output reasoning. Dictionary-based sparse KV compression is appealing because it represents each token's KV states as a sparse combination of learned basis elements (atoms) in a dictionary, preserving token-level structure without enforcing a global low-rank constraint. However, existing dictionary-based methods face two practical limitations during decoding: iterative sparse coding methods such as Orthogonal Matching Pursuit add decoding-time overhead, and static dictionaries may mismatch evolving KV distributions under test-time generation. While adaptive dictionary updates can reduce this mismatch, naive append-only strategies lead to dictionary growth during generation. We propose FAD-KV, a \textbf{F}ast \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{D}ictionary KV caching framework for efficient reasoning decoding. FAD-KV integrates (i) a screening-based sparse coding scheme, where a one-shot Top-\(n\) selection identifies a small candidate pool followed by lightweight coefficient refinement, and (ii) a fixed-size dictionary adaptation mechanism that reuses inactive atoms instead of expanding the dictionary. By controlling both sparse coding cost and adaptive dictionary growth, FAD-KV provides a memory-bounded and computation-efficient KV compression framework for long reasoning workloads. Experiments on reasoning and long-context benchmarks show that FAD-KV achieves a strong quality-efficiency trade-off while avoiding unbounded dictionary growth during decoding.

Title: Sometimes less is more: Investigating inductive biases of MLPs and Transformers on noisy k-parity features

  • Authors: Alasdair Paren
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37976
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37976

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: We empirically investigate how MLPs and Transformers differ when trained on data sets containing one or more features encoded by noisy k-parity. Noisy k-parity gives us hard synthetic data sets in which we can control the predictivity and availability of every feature. Across a range of data distributions, data scales and model sizes, we find large differences in the training dynamics and inductive biases of the two architectures: Transformers are more robust to noisy features but struggle to learn complex, low-availability features, whereas MLPs show the opposite trend and improve steadily with more data. Title: Hierarchical Diffusion for Camera-controlled Autoregressive Video Generation

  • Abstract: We address the task of long-video, camera-controlled generation from a single image. Standard solutions to both--such as self-forcing and conditioning on explicit 6-DOF poses--either incur significant computational overhead or produce drifting scene geometries over time. Experiments on RealEstate10k and DL3DV demonstrate that WanAR+FixAR outperforms prior methods for camera-controlled long-video generation, producing results that are both temporally stable and consistent with input camera trajectories.

  • Authors: George Ruellan, Diana Benavides-Prado

  • Abstract: Benign fine-tuning can erode safety alignment in large language models, and sequential adaptation substantially compounds this risk.   • Keywords: privacy, fair, generative
  • Abstract: Bounds based on Integral Probability Metrics (IPMs) are ubiquitous in machine learning and statistics, appearing, e.g., in generalization, alignment, fairness, and privacy. IPMs reduce the discrepancy between two distributions to a single worst-case gap between expectations. This compression is costly: for rare events, safety failures, or under-represented groups, IPM-based bounds are loose or vacuous. We introduce a non-linear generalization of IPMs, which we term Integral Probability Boundaries (IPBs). Unlike IPMs, IPBs provide bounds that are tight at the extremes. We derive their basic properties and present algorithms for estimating them in practically relevant settings. We demonstrate the usage of IPBs in both theoretical and practical applications for alignment, judge-free performance estimation, and fairness, where they enable us to obtain sharp bounds on performance and fairness gaps, e.g., 15 p.p. more precise estimates on fairness gaps for rare groups in a realistic generative modeling task, compared to the state of the art.

Title: Measuring Deep Learning Performance - An Empirical Study of Performance Distributions Across Architectures and Tasks

  • Authors: Kevin L Coakley, Odd Erik Gundersen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38037
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38037
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38037]] Measuring Deep Learning Performance - An Empirical Study of Performance Distributions Across Architectures and Tasks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38037)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Context: Non-determinism in deep learning algorithm design and implementation leads to performance variation, meaning model performance is not a single value, but rather a distribution. These model performance distributions are underexplored despite their impact on robustness. Objective: We investigate the robustness of deep learning performance to sources of non-determinism, specifically focusing on how performance distributions differ across various architectures and tasks. Method: We conducted 186 experiments on image classification and time series forecasting architectures. Each experiment was run 100 times with different random seeds to generate performance distributions, resulting in 18,600 runs. Robustness was quantified using metrics for spread, symmetry, and tail risk. Results: Performance distributions are frequently non-Gaussian, particularly in time series forecasting. Model size does not systematically affect robustness -- larger image classification models show fewer outliers but not lower spread, while smaller time series models show lower spread but more extreme underperformers. Training duration does not scale linearly; early stopping effectively balances performance and robustness. Mean performance does not predict robustness - time series forecasting shows moderate correlation while image classification shows none. Time series models produce nearly three times more underperforming outliers than image classification models, indicating substantially higher tail risk. Conclusions: Tail risk poses serious concerns for Trustworthy AI in high-stakes applications. Models performing well on average may exhibit long tails and extreme outliers revealed only through distributional analysis. Mean performance alone should not guide model selection; assessment of spread, symmetry, and tail risk is essential for reliable model assessment where consistent performance is critical.

  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge has become a standard for evaluating large language models, yet most approaches implicitly assume that a single judge prompt functions as a stable evaluation instrument. In contrast, we investigate whether validated prompt variants intended to preserve evaluation semantics while differing in surface realization (\textit{paraphrastic rephrasings}) yield consistent judgments under a fixed evaluation task. We introduce \textit{Prompt Lottery}, a phenomenon in which paraphrastic rephrasings produce different LLMaJ judgments. A user study with 10 human annotators shows that LLMaJ judges exhibit 6-9$\times$ larger agreement degradation under prompt rephrasings than humans, falling below the generally accepted threshold in usability studies. This indicates that single-prompt evaluation is insufficiently robust for model ranking. Finally, we show that multi-prompt aggregation substantially improves robustness by emphasizing judgments that remain consistent across prompt variants.

Title: CADENCE: Distributional Next-Transaction Forecasting for Business Cash-Flow Underwriting

  • Authors: Alexander Wu, Jingchao Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, q-fin.RM

  • Abstract: Recurring cash flows are the backbone of cash-flow underwriting: they separate durable revenue from one-off deposits, expose standing obligations, and ultimately determine ability to pay.   • Authors: Darsh Asher, Farshad Dizani, Joshua Kalyanapu, Ro Cammarota, Aydin Aysu, Samira Mirbagher Ajorpaz
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38110
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38110

  • Abstract: Deep neural networks (DNNs) are widely deployed in safety- and privacy-critical domains such as healthcare and finance, where models are trained on confidential data. By contrast, a padding defense that equalizes responses to the worst-case time masks timing leakage with only 7.24% average performance overhead across nine libraries on Texas-100X/Adult and near-zero extra energy.

Title: A Separation Principle for Adversarial Attacks and Defense of Graph Neural Networks

  • Authors: Farid Mafi, Saber Jafarpour, Mohammad Pirani
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38127
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38127
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38127]] A Separation Principle for Adversarial Attacks and Defense of Graph Neural Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38127)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Adversarial attacks on graph neural networks (GNNs) have been studied as a model-centric optimization problem driven by gradients, learned policies, or meta-learning, with the graph treated as a passive substrate. This view overlooks a defining feature of GNNs: their predictions are produced by structured message passing along the graph, so their adversarial vulnerabilities are encoded as much in the geometry of the graph as in the learned weights. We develop a structure-driven framework for feature attacks in which the attack is derived from the spectral properties of the normalized adjacency operator. Its central result is a separation principle: under a natural relaxation of the attack objective, the optimal feature perturbation is rank one and factorizes into a graph-dependent component (degree-proportional spatial allocation, depending only on the graph) and a weight-dependent component (top singular vector of the composed weights, depending only on the weights). Node degree thus emerges as the fundamental graph quantity governing adversarial vulnerability to feature perturbations. The resulting attack, which we call SepAttack, requires no gradient computation, carries analytical guarantees, achieves competitive or superior performance against established baselines such as Nettack and IG-Attack with up to ${\sim}70\times$ speedup in the targeted setting, and transfers near-perfectly across architectures on continuous-feature graphs because its graph-dependent component is architecture-invariant by construction. We further derive a separation-principle adversarial training procedure (SepAT) directly from the closed-form worst-case attack and report robustness gains on binary-feature benchmarks.

Title: Replaying the Same Gradients at a Different $\varepsilon$: A Registered Test of the Fixed-Trajectory Account of Adam's $\varepsilon$ for One Rare Row

  • Authors: Lngel Kyo (A2O Labs)
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38169
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38169
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38169]] Replaying the Same Gradients at a Different $\varepsilon$: A Registered Test of the Fixed-Trajectory Account of Adam's $\varepsilon$ for One Rare Row(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38169)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Adam divides every update by the square root of a running average of squared gradients plus a small constant, epsilon. For an output-embedding row whose token is rare, which we call the probe row, raising epsilon can be read as shrinking that row's steps by arithmetic alone. We test that reading directly. While a training run proceeds, we also divide the probe row's update by a different epsilon, using the run's own moment estimates; this replay is exact arithmetic on the gradients the run actually saw, so it holds the run's trajectory fixed. A rule for when the replay counts as wrong was written down before the runs. We train a small causal transformer in a standard configuration. When epsilon rises by four orders of magnitude, the replay counts as wrong on each of the three seeds registered for the test: the probe row keeps 8.9--11.4 times more of its displacement than predicted with the main, sampled-negative head, and 5.0--7.7 times more with a full-softmax head scored as a second instrument. At three orders of magnitude, tested with the main head only, it does not, by narrow margins; a further two seeds, registered after this result had been committed and scored on their own, follow the same pattern. In separate real runs, raising epsilon on the probe row alone is enough to reproduce the row's change and raising it on every other parameter alone also moves the row part of the way --- readings about the runs that do not rescue the replay; scaling the row's learning rate instead, with the denominator untouched, is mispredicted by the replay in the same direction. This is one embedding row of one model family on one corpus; we make no claim about rates, about rows in general (other rows were read only as a panel on one trajectory) or about how the effect changes with model size.

  • Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) allows for secure analysis of distributed data without data sharing. We and others have successfully applied FL to neuroimaging tasks, such as Alzheimer's disease classification from structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). FL trains a deep learning model locally at each site, encrypts the local model parameters and sends them to a federation controller that aggregates the local models under homomorphic encryption, the resulting community model is sent back to the sites for further training, and the process repeats for a number of rounds. FL often achieves learning performance (e.g., accuracy) close to centralized training, but no data leaves the sites and model parameters are protected through encryption and other measures. In this paper, we show that federations composed of sites with heterogeneous amounts of data (1 large site plus small sites) perform better than homogeneous (same data per site) federations, particularly in low data regimes, while reaching performance close to centralized training. Having a big site often results in faster convergence and higher final accuracy. Heterogeneous federations better exploit sites with small amounts of data, a common situation in large consortia such as ENIGMA.

Title: A Gradient based Adversarial Augmentation Framework for Mitigating Unlearnable Datasets

  • Authors: Long Dang, Satpal Sura, Kaiqi Xiong
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38173
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38173
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38173]] A Gradient based Adversarial Augmentation Framework for Mitigating Unlearnable Datasets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38173)
  • Keywords: protect, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Unlearnable data methods (ULD) protect public data from unauthorized model training by adding imperceptible perturbations that create a learning shortcut. A deep neural network can exploit the shortcut learning path to minimize training loss without learning generalizable features. Intuitively, finding adversarial versions of perturbed images that can substantially increase the model's loss can force the model to learn generalizable features. Existing data augmentation based attacks rely on heuristic search strategies/random sampling over a curated augmentation set to identify adversarial augmentation operations, an approach that often does not scale when the number of candidate operations is large. To address the challenge of a large search space, we propose {\techname}, a gradient based adversarial augmentation framework that learns an augmentation policy via stochastic gradient ascent. {\techname} leverages a differentiable data augmentation pipeline to efficiently search a set of up to 18 augmentation techniques, from which a grid search selects a protection-specific subset. The augmentation subset is trained to maximize a surrogate model's classification loss. We introduce a perceptual-plus-pixel regularization to learn operation parameters that preserve image semantics. Under a controlled ablation, {\techname} consistently outperforms an equivalent random sampling baseline (80.0\% vs. 77.29\% clean test accuracy on EMIN-protected CIFAR-10 for ResNet-18 and 83.28\% vs. 66.45\% on NTGA-protected CIFAR-10 for MobileNet-V2), demonstrating that gradient guidance drives the clean test accuracy recoverability rather than the expanded search space. {\techname} offers an effective and scalable adversarial augmentation framework for stress-testing ULD methods, a critical tool for developing robust ULD methods.

OCR preserves the text signal that guarantees recall, while the MLLM jointly performs OCR-grounded discrimination and temporal grounding, localizing both textual and non-textual inducements. We construct a dataset of 2,419 internet finance short videos (66.49 hours) with 3,194 annotated events across four categories, and study reward design for RL-based grounding under class imbalance. We report two findings: auxiliary reward terms are exploited through reward hacking, and stronger localization rewards degrade class-balanced detection. We therefore design a recall-weighted F2 localization reward with class-balanced resampling. Experiments show OCR+MLLM localizes better than the keyframe OCR+LLM baseline (mIoU 26.52 vs. 14.49) and detects non-textual inducements that text-only pipelines miss, while maintaining 67.0 percent macro recall.

Title: Progressive Coupling Method for Diffusion Posterior Sampling in Ptychography

  • Authors: Yihan Qiu, Mingtao Huang, Tianyuan Li, Shudong Li, Yuan Shen
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38207
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38207
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38207]] Progressive Coupling Method for Diffusion Posterior Sampling in Ptychography(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38207)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion
  • Abstract: Ptychography is a computational imaging method that reconstructs a sample from overlapping diffraction measurements. This technique relies on these overlapping measurements to provide the redundancy information needed for robust reconstruction, but dense scanning increases acquisition time and data volume. Reducing scan overlaps can improve measurement efficiency but weakens the physical constraints available for reconstruction. Diffusion models provide strong learned prior information, offering a promising approach to compensate for the loss of measurement redundancy. However, effectively utilizing this prior information under weak measurement constraints remains a challenge. We propose a progressive coupling posterior sampling (PCPS) method, which allows for a broader exploration of the solution space under high uncertainty while maintaining stronger diffusion-guided consistency as the reconstruction becomes more refined. Experiments on real X-ray ptychographic data at various scan overlaps demonstrate that PCPS consistently improves reconstruction accuracy compared to traditional iterative methods and existing diffusion posterior samplers, especially at low overlaps. Title: Is it my preference? Role Grounding Attack against Agent's Memory via Social Interaction

  • Authors: Haoran Bu, Yiting Shen, Litian Zhang, Ruihan Hu, Xi Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38209
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38209
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38209]] Is it my preference? Role Grounding Attack against Agent's Memory via Social Interaction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38209)
  • Keywords: privacy, attack
  • Abstract: LLM-based agents now share experiences on social platforms and remember new preferences to enhance their performance. However, existing memory systems contain a vulnerability in role-to-principal attribution that attackers can exploit. We identify Role Grounding Misattribution, in which an attacker-designed interaction causes the victim agent to incorrectly record a preference from the attacker as a preference of the victim's user. Specifically, we are the first to formalize Role Grounding Attack (RGA), an attack model that exploits this vulnerability through nested conversation structures. In the memory formation stage, an experience-sharing post quoting a conversation between the attacker agent and its user is published, which explicitly identifies its publisher. The inner conversation expresses preferences from the user of the attacker agent. When the victim agent remembers preferences from this nested structure, its memory loses the context binding and grounds the attacker's preference to the victim's user instead. In the memory retrieval stage, the misattributed preference persists and influences the victim agent's decisions when it encounters relevant scenarios. We instantiate RGA as tool selection manipulation and privacy leakage. Tool selection manipulation causes a tool-call preference to be misattributed to the victim's user, steering the victim toward an attacker-controlled tool. Privacy leakage extends this by misattributing both a tool-call preference and a task-requirement preference to the victim's user. The victim selects the attacker's tool, then includes sensitive information in tool arguments to satisfy the task requirement. We evaluate RGA across five mainstream memory systems and six language models. Tool manipulation and privacy leakage both achieve high attack success rates. These results call for stronger role grounding.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Large-scale PDE pre-training trains a single model on the data from multiple partial differential equations (PDEs). However, adding heterogeneous PDEs with different governing equations, physical variables, and variable interactions does not always improve predictive performance across tasks. Existing PDE mixture-of-experts models mitigate cross-task interference through learned routing, but typically use homogeneous expert architectures, making it difficult to directly reflect differences in state evolution across equations. We introduce HotPOT, an operator transformer with heterogeneous physics experts for large-scale PDE pre-training. HotPOT uses a shared encoder to capture information common across PDEs, while each PDE family uses a neural-numerical expert that pairs a learnable predictor with a fixed numerical update containing no trainable parameters. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: ParaTTT: Parallelizing Test-Time Training Layers with Deep Memories

  • Authors: Noel Loo, Neehal Tumma, T. Konstantin Rusch, Mathias Lechner, Alexander Amini, Daniela Rus
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38255
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38255
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38255]] ParaTTT: Parallelizing Test-Time Training Layers with Deep Memories(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38255)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Test-time training (TTT) layers replace the linear fast-weight memory of modern recurrent architectures with a small neural network trained online by gradient descent. This nonlinear memory is more expressive than the linear memories of Gated DeltaNet and Mamba-2, but is difficult to parallelize: existing implementations such as TTT-MLP rely on sequential, block-wise weight updates that run several times slower than chunkwise-parallel linear recurrences. Moreover, their block-wise execution only approximates the token-by-token online recurrence, whereas linear recurrences admit an exact chunkwise-parallel evaluation. We introduce ParaTTT (PTTT), an algorithm for parallelizing arbitrarily deep nonlinear memories by iteratively refining estimates of the inner-loop gradient trajectory. Starting from a guess of the hidden-layer gradients, each refinement consists of three chunkwise-parallel passes: a forward pass that evaluates the resulting feature trajectory, an exact gated-delta-rule pass that computes the readout trajectory and its feature gradients, and a backward pass that propagates these gradients into a new estimate. The exact online gradient descent recurrence is a fixed point of the iteration, while decoding requires no refinement iterations. We prove geometric convergence for a two-layer variant under sufficient contraction conditions. In practice, two iterations suffice. With two refinement steps at 4K context, PTTT evaluates the inner-loop trajectory $32\times$ faster than TTT-MLP with the block approximation at matched error, while the full 340M model trains $4.9\times$ faster. Models trained at 340M and 1.3B parameters match or exceed Gated DeltaNet and Mamba-2 and remain competitive with same-size Transformers on language-modeling perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, with better recall than the linear recurrences.

Title: Rosemary: Efficient and Adaptable In-Storage Acceleration for Fully Homomorphic Encryption

  • Authors: Xuan Wang, Tianqi Zhang, Gabrielle De Micheli, Yujin Nam, Keming Fan, Augusto Vega, Minxuan Zhou, Tajana Rosing
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38262

  • Abstract: Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables computation on encrypted data and is promising for privacy-preserving computing.   • Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) achieve strong image recognition performance, but their self-attention increases quadratically with the number of input tokens and makes them computationally expensive. In this paper, we propose ConvRoute-ViT, a convolutional token routing mechanism for efficient vision transformers that performs token reduction through convolutional routing. This diagnostic process constitutes a complete chain of reasoning. We present Fu-TCM, a multimodal TCM LLM trained on data retained after cleaning and filtering an initial collection of approximately 7.12 million examples derived from classical texts, textbooks, medical images, clinical cases and publicly available datasets. The model was further optimized using Bianzheng-Grounded Policy Optimization (BGPO), which explicitly targets syndrome differentiation through rewards for response format, intermediate TCM reasoning fields and final syndrome predictions. Fu-TCM-27B led all six benchmarks spanning TCM reasoning, text and vision, achieving a macro-average accuracy of 81.01% and surpassing the strongest reference model with complete results across all six benchmarks by 7.13 percentage points. Its largest margin was 11.90 points on Fu-bianzheng. Case-level analyses further showed a positive association between intermediate-field accuracy and final syndrome-selection accuracy in most evaluated models. These findings suggest that TCM syndrome differentiation may be computationally learnable and that structured reasoning supervision can improve performance across TCM tasks.

Title: Learning a Prior-Data Fitted Network for Graph Foundation Models via Structural Causal Model Priors

  • Abstract: Sparse-view cone-beam computed tomography is widely used for fast, low-dose volumetric imaging and lesion localization in dentistry and image-guided interventions, but it introduces severe artifacts. However, information from the whole scan is written only when the field is constructed and cannot be retrieved during coordinate decoding, leaving the volume inconsistent within the scan. This is a common limitation of such methods: once the field is fixed, decoding uses only local features and reconstruction quality drops substantially. Experiments on volcanic rock and chicken show performance after same-object adaptation. A reusable token pool gives feed-forward reconstruction a mechanism that keeps scan-wide context available through decoding and can benefit future rapid clinical lesion localization and analysis. Title: TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering
