  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38366
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38366]] TCC-RAG: Temporal Causal Chain-Guided RAG for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38366)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred growing interest in temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). Most existing methods rely on semantic retrieval augmented generation (RAG), which suffers from fundamental limitations in complex temporal reasoning. On the one hand, semantic-based RAG methods struggle to capture the multi-hop causal knowledge required for complex temporal reasoning. On the other hand, these methods introduce excessive redundant knowledge, trapping LLMs in spurious reasoning chains that potentially result in erroneous answers. To address these problems, we propose a Temporal Causal Chain-guided RAG (TCC-RAG) to retrieve a concise yet sufficient set of causal knowledge to support complex temporal reasoning. Specifically, we reformulate the temporal knowledge graph into a multi-view causal cue graph, with causal cues inducing connections that explicitly encode temporal evolution and causal dynamics. Based on the causal cue graph, we construct a query-specific temporal causal chain to guide the retrieval process, enabling the model to focus on a concise set of causally relevant knowledge. Finally, we introduce a causal evidence filter to refine the retrieved knowledge, ensuring its causal consistency with the underlying temporal logic of the query. Experiments show that TCC-RAG achieves substantial performance gains of 10.72% and 8.40% on two TKGQA benchmarks, and delivers a remarkable 62.97% improvement for multi-step temporal reasoning.

Title: Deeper Backpropagation or More Updates? Training Looped Language Models Across Budgets

  • Authors: Jiarun Shen, Xiao Li
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38422
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38422
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38422]] Deeper Backpropagation or More Updates? Training Looped Language Models Across Budgets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38422)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Repeating transformer layers can improve language model performance without adding parameters. However, the added computational depth increases both forward and backward computation costs during training. Training such models requires choosing a backward window that controls how many final layer repetitions retain gradients during backpropagation. Longer windows improve per-token learning efficiency but increase per-update cost, while shorter windows reduce per-update cost but sacrifice per-token efficiency. Understanding which window performs better requires examining different training budgets. Prior work compares backward windows across computational budgets. When training budgets increase, it remains unclear whether the same window continues to perform best. We measure how the relative performance of two windows shifts as training budgets grow. Our method isolates per-token efficiency by training both windows on identical data, and compares complete training recipes at approximately equal training time at each budget. Results show that shorter windows lose much of their advantage as budgets grow. Controlled experiments show that both benefits decrease at larger budgets, with a greater decline in the benefit of extended training. Backward window comparisons depend on the available budget, and the preferred window among the tested candidates can change as the budget grows.

Title: Leaving the Cave by Breaking the Symmetry: Identifiable Structure Can Enable Cross-Lingual Generalization

  • Authors: Frederick Riemenschneider, Anette Frank
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38509
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38509
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38509]] Leaving the Cave by Breaking the Symmetry: Identifiable Structure Can Enable Cross-Lingual Generalization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38509)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Why do multilingual language models generalize across languages even without shared vocabulary? The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different data, objectives, and modalities converge toward a shared statistical model of reality. Extending this intuition to representations inside a single multilingual model, we propose the Relational Identifiability Hypothesis: relations in a shared world can promote entity alignment by ruling out alternative cross-lingual correspondences, making corresponding entities more likely to become directly readable without an additional transformation. We test this hypothesis in a controlled bilingual Transformer whose two token-disjoint languages describe the same modular-arithmetic world. We find that the languages can develop a shared answer subspace without aligning corresponding numbers. These patterns predict success in zero-shot cross-lingual transfer. Together, our results show that relational structure in a shared world can promote entity alignment and enable transfer.

Title: Similar Scores, Opposite Failures: Human Correction as an Instrument for Video Caption Evaluation

  • Authors: Ankit Khedia, Astitva Jaiswal, Chaitanya Prasad, Anisha Raju, Aangeeras
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38511
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38511
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38511]] Similar Scores, Opposite Failures: Human Correction as an Instrument for Video Caption Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38511)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Video captioning models are increasingly evaluated with metrics designed for single-sentence image captions. We test whether those metrics recover human judgement on dense, multi-window video description, and find that they do not. Six frontier models captioned 196 short clips. Trained annotators post-edited every caption against a failure taxonomy fixed before annotation, once as a whole-clip overview and once window by window, and a quality-control reviewer checked each annotation, yielding 1,144 overview verdicts and 4,672 window verdicts. Reference-overlap metrics do not separate the captions reviewers accepted from those they rejected: BLEU-4 reaches an AUC of 0.510 and a CIDEr-style tf-idf cosine 0.528, and restricting references to reviewer corrections leaves BLEU-4 at 0.479 (95% CI 0.431-0.529), indistinguishable from chance. Review granularity changes what is detected. The same annotators record camera and shot errors on 2.7% of captions when reading the overview and on 12.8% when reading window by window, a 4.7x gap that holds across every robustness check we ran, on the mode most likely to need a near-total rewrite. Correct-overview rates span 54.1-72.7%, but only 4 of 15 pairwise gaps survive Bonferroni correction (5 after adjusting for reviewer and clip effects), and accuracy and derived severity order the models differently. Models with statistically indistinguishable accuracy have sharply different failure profiles, which no single scalar shows. We argue that caption evaluation should record what was wrong rather than only how often, and we report the taxonomy, the per-model profiles, the corpus properties that predict difficulty, and the code that regenerates every number in the paper.

Title: The Power Watershed as a Differentiable Segmentation Layer

  • Authors: Galia Akl (AUB), Laurent Najman (LIGM, KUSTAR), Hugues Talbot (OPIS, CVN)
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.NE, eess.IV, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38516
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38516
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38516]] The Power Watershed as a Differentiable Segmentation Layer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38516)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Can a Watershed transform, the classical tool of morphological segmentation, be placed at the end of a deep network and trained through? The Power Watershed (PW) is the limit of the seeded Random Walker (RW) as p grows to infinity with edge weights raised to the power p. At every finite p the RW solves a sparse positive-definite linear system and is exactly differentiable with respect to the edge weights and the unaries through one adjoint solve; its multi-label form costs one right-hand side per label, and its limit is the PW. We study this family as the last layer of a segmentation network, the watershed layer (WS layer). A conditioning study measures how the solve, the gradient and the distance to the limit evolve with p, and the implicit gradient is checked against unrolling. On synthetic data with correct edge costs the WS layer transforms a soft prediction into an exact boundary. On nuclei segmentation (PanNuke), a binary WS layer is level with a thresholded U-Net. With the instances as labels, the WS layer turns the U-Net into an instance segmenter whose number of labels is set per image by the markers rather than by the output layout, and the network is trained through that partition: instance F1 score rises from 0.656 to 0.795 over a U-Net with connected components, level with the same network followed by a tuned marker watershed. At the training temperature the partition is a watershed of the learned costs, and the finite temperature lets the solve hedge, discounting weak markers and sharing contested pixels. The hedging puts the WS layer 0.015--0.020 F1 ahead of the baseline when trained on a tenth of the data, at the price of having to deploy the finite-p solve rather than its exact limit at inference. Code and the full experimental record are released.

Title: AtomSteer: Test-Time Constraint Steering for Atomistic Generative Models

  • Authors: Pengfei Cai, Katharina S. C. Jäger, Utkarsh Utkarsh, Nofit Segal, Bowen Deng, Akshay Subramanian, Rafael Gomez-Bombarelli
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38517
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38517
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38517]] AtomSteer: Test-Time Constraint Steering for Atomistic Generative Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38517)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion and flow matching models offer scalable approaches to generating crystalline materials, molecular conformers, and protein-ligand complexes. However, satisfying crystallographic symmetry, structural geometry, and accurate stereochemistry remains challenging. Satisfying these requirements calls for corrections that preserve the geometry already captured by the pretrained model. In this work, we propose AtomSteer, a test-time inference framework for steering pretrained atomistic generative models with structural constraints. During sampling, AtomSteer corrects intermediate structure predictions with bounded local projections, graph-rigid stereochemical transformations, and projections over periodic symmetries, accepting updates that improve constraint satisfaction while limiting changes to the rest of the structure. We demonstrate AtomSteer across three pretrained backbones, FlowMM, ET-Flow, and Boltz-2, spanning de novo crystal generation and crystal structure prediction, molecular conformer generation, and protein-ligand complex structure prediction. AtomSteer improves space-group distribution coverage in de novo crystal generation and improves target space-group recovery in crystal structure prediction. AtomSteer improves molecular validity and E/Z stereochemical accuracy while retaining conformer ensemble fidelity. For protein-ligand complexes, AtomSteer reduces ligand E/Z errors relative to experimental reference structures while achieving competitive docking success and PoseBusters validity. Our method provides a unified framework for adding chemical and crystallographic control to existing atomistic generative models without retraining or architectural changes.

Title: Self-Consistency Geometry Injection for Multi-view Consistent Image Generation

  • Authors: Yupeng Xiao, Yankai Wu, Jianjie Luo, Jiajie Lin, Yi Yu, Zhenguo Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.GR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38518
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38518
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38518]] Self-Consistency Geometry Injection for Multi-view Consistent Image Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38518)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Recent diffusion models have achieved impressive progress in single image to multi-view generation by synthesizing consistent novel views efficiently. Existing methods usually explicitly or implicitly couple geometry inference and texture synthesis, making generation vulnerable to inaccurate geometry prediction. Although several geometry-guided methods have explored decoupling, they usually treat predicted geometry as a fixed condition and do not explicitly model whether the geometry is reliable. We therefore propose Reliability-Guided Multi-View Generation (RGMV), which comprises a parameter-free Self-Consistency Geometry Gate (SCG) and a Reliability-aware Geometry Injection (RGI) module. Specifically, a geometry proposal generator produces multiple geometry predictions for the same reference image and target viewpoints under different stochastic sampling configurations. SCG measures their spatial agreement to derive a reliability map, which RGI uses to modulate the injection strength of geometry-conditioned residuals in the RGB synthesis model. Moreover, RGMV can adapt to different geometry proposal generators. As a result, RGMV preserves stronger geometric guidance in stable regions while attenuating unreliable geometry cues before they propagate into multi-view RGB generation. Extensive experiments show that reliability-aware geometry injection improves the geometric quality of multi-view generation. Code: this https URL.

Title: What Hallucinations Have We Measured? Semantic Illusion, Confabulation, and Expressed Uncertainty

  • Authors: Hazel H. Kim, Jakub Binkowski, Ekaterina Fadeeva, Juan David Guerra, Yijie Hao, Zheng Yi Ho, Baixiang Huang, Buyun Liang, Siyuan Liang, Anh Tuan Luu, Potsawee Manakul, Niels Mündler-Sasahara, Aidar Myrzakhan, Shahrad Mohammadzadeh, Artem Shelmanov, Kai Shu, Ponhvoan Srey, Dacheng Tao, René Vidal, Haofei Yu, Lei Yu, Hongxiang Zhang, Yarin Gal, Hinrich Schütze, Philip H.S. Torr
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38520
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38520
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38520]] What Hallucinations Have We Measured? Semantic Illusion, Confabulation, and Expressed Uncertainty(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38520)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Hallucination remains one of the most critical challenges in deploying large language models (LLMs) reliably and safely. Yet the term is used inconsistently across the research community. We argue for a strict definition of hallucinations in LLMs. First, hallucinations should be confident incorrectness, kept separate from expressed uncertainty (UNK)1. UNK reveals that the model may be wrong, whereas a hallucination does not reveal it and is therefore more dangerous in practice. Conflating the two obscures their distinct behaviors and makes them hard to study. Second, hallucinations should be split by a simple re-elicitation test: does any semantically equivalent prompt recover the correct response? This test probes whether the relevant knowledge2 is accessible. If none does, the knowledge is likely absent or inaccessible, and the error type is a confabulation (CF) that likely requires external knowledge or retraining; if some does, the knowledge is accessible from the model but applied inconsistently, and the error type is a semantic illusion (SI) that simple prompt or decoding changes can mitigate3. Re-evaluating 14 recent hallucination methods under this taxonomy yields three findings. (1) Detection methods flag UNK more often than hallucinated responses (i.e., SI and CF). (2) Mitigation methods are far more effective for SI than for CF. (3) Latent signals are more informative than output-level signals for CF. An audit of recent hallucination papers shows that most conflate these categories. We recommend reporting CF, SI, and UNK separately, and introduce a practical labeling protocol that needs only a modest number of semantically equivalent prompts.

Title: WebMaze: Defending Against LLM-Powered Web Scanners with Functionality-Coupled Deception

  • Authors: Pengfei Zeng, Changhua Luo, Penghui Li
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38524
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38524
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38524]] WebMaze: Defending Against LLM-Powered Web Scanners with Functionality-Coupled Deception(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38524)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have enabled more effective vulnerability scanning of web applications. However, these capabilities also make it significantly easier for attackers to automate cyberattacks on the web. In this work, we investigate how to divert malicious LLM-based web scanning, with preservation of genuine functionality as a deployment requirement. We systematically study six LLM-based scanners and summarize how they gather information and iteratively select actions based on observations. Guided by the study, we then present WebMaze, a defense that preserves genuine application functionality while diverting LLM-based scanners to a generated decoy world. WebMaze embeds functionality-coupled traps in genuine pages and constructs view-tailored interactive decoy worlds from the application's existing views. The traps blend into genuine components and expose entry points to decoy worlds that sustain exploration through connected pages and interactions without changing genuine functionality. Across 12 web applications and three LLM-based scanners under the default no-disclosure setting, decoy pages account for an average of 93.0% of the distinct pages requested per run. This demonstrates that WebMaze effectively diverts scanners away from genuine application pages and into the generated decoy worlds. Even when explicitly informed about the decoy mechanism and instructed to avoid decoy pages, scanners continue to explore predominantly decoy pages.

Title: Implicit Minor Detection from Multi-Turn Dialogue in High-Risk AI Interactions: A Constrained Self-Evolving LLM Harness

  • Authors: Xiaohan Zhang, Ying Zhao, Ruilin Guo, Xuanyuan Yu, Kaibo Huang, Yukun Wei, Zhongliang Yang, Linna Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38546
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38546
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38546]] Implicit Minor Detection from Multi-Turn Dialogue in High-Risk AI Interactions: A Constrained Self-Evolving LLM Harness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38546)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In high-risk text interactions, a system may need to estimate whether the current speaker is likely to be a minor before applying age-conditioned safeguards. Many text-based dialogue systems lack strong identity signals such as account information, verified identity, facial data, or device data, and must rely on weak cues that emerge across multiple turns. We formulate implicit minor detection under weak identity information as a high-risk user-attribute inference task and propose a constrained framework comprising a large language model, a Harness, and a Maker-Checker protocol. The framework controls when inference is invoked, requires structured dialogue-linked evidence, and subjects validation-selected Harness revisions to version-level review. On a controlled Chinese benchmark comprising 8,328 multi-turn dialogues, our method improves average precision and minor-class F1 by 7.60 and 6.37 percentage points, respectively, over direct inference using the same LLM family.

Title: Detecting Fine-Grained Differences between Image Datasets

  • Authors: Leander Girrbach, Alexander von Recum, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38547
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38547
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38547]] Detecting Fine-Grained Differences between Image Datasets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38547)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Detecting whether and in particular how two data distributions differ is a fundamental statistical problem. For example, we might be interested in which concepts (expressed in natural language) differ between two classes, the outputs of different generative models, or the same model with different prompt specifications. When working with large and complex image datasets, we intuitively think that first selecting concepts from a broad list using fine-grained patch embeddings, and then optimizing the concepts to better fit the data is an effective workflow. However, prior work instead uses MLLMs to propose concepts and image-level embeddings to select them. Therefore, we systematically compare these techniques and find that selecting concepts from broad lists can replace MLLM-proposals at obviously lower cost. MLLM inference can then be spent on optimizing selected concepts instead to achieve substantial improvements. Additionally, by designing a new benchmark that focuses on low-prevalence and multi-concept distinctions, we find that selecting nonredundant concepts instead of only the most distinguishing concepts matters, and we introduce techniques for how to effectively do this. Finally, we show how our methods address important problems like detecting spurious correlations and biases. Overall, our work presents a fundamental perspective shift on how we analyze interpretable distributional differences between datasets.

Title: A Minimally Informed Black-Box Fake-Report Attack on DRL Traffic-Signal Control

  • Authors: Amro Saleh, Loukas Lazos, Mohammad J. Abdel-Rahman
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38557
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38557
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38557]] A Minimally Informed Black-Box Fake-Report Attack on DRL Traffic-Signal Control(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38557)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) traffic-signal controllers may use vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) reports, exposing their perception layer to false-data injection. Existing demonstrations typically assume either white-box access to the controller's weights or full observability of the traffic state. We study the impact of a weaker and more realistic adversary who has no access to the RL model and whose traffic observations are limited to her own approach. This black-box, minimally informed attacker trains an imitation surrogate of the controller. She then crafts bounded fake reports, restricted to inflating vehicle counts on her own approach, that transfer to the real controller. We demonstrate that despite the loss of accuracy relative to an attacker who can observe the entire state and action space, this limited attacker can significantly increase the average waiting time for other approaches while barely changing the waiting time on her own approach, i.e. externalizing the added delay onto the other approaches. Because the only realizable move is to inflate one's own reported queue, we find that a zero-knowledge attacker that simply reports the maximum feasible queue is as damaging as the surrogate-based one, while an unstructured random inflation is not: the surrogate is not required to reproduce the degradation, and the exposed vulnerability is the V2I sensing channel itself. The surrogate's role is directional control, selecting a disruptive or a selfish objective at the same budget. The attack persists under +/-20% uniform demand variation. Finally, on a common per-lane budget we place the attacker on a knowledge ladder against white-box adversaries, and find the black-box surrogate matches a realizable white-box attacker that uses the victim's own gradients; only abandoning realizability raises the damage further.

Title: Episodic looking - using reinforcement learning to bias perception enhances few-shot and continual learning with protection from forgetting

  • Authors: David Rawlinson, Gideon Kowadlo
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38577
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38577
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38577]] Episodic looking - using reinforcement learning to bias perception enhances few-shot and continual learning with protection from forgetting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38577)
  • Keywords: protect, robust
  • Abstract: There is a fundamental contradiction between the desire for models that can generalize, and models that can continuously adapt to recent input with changing statistics. These conditions, captured in few-shot and continual learning settings, can bias or disrupt learned representations. This harms generalization to a broader context, leaving the model unable to overcome input variance. This paper proposes a solution based on the Complementary Learning System architecture (CLS), in which a Short-Term Memory (STM) delegates generalization to a slow, statistical Long-Term Memory (LTM). Delegation is achieved by working in a space of robust, generalized perceptions produced by the LTM. With this separation, the STM can focus on memorizing recent input and avoiding interference between memories. We show that on an image classification task, the resulting CLS model is more resistant to forgetting in a domain-incremental continual learning setting than a baseline ResNet LTM, and generalizes more effectively in a few-shot setting than other continual learners. In the continual setting, overall accuracy is slightly worse than existing continual learning models, but with a different mix of performance characteristics. Results analysis suggests that the model is limited by the representations and representational plasticity of the LTM. In future work, we will seek to address this by replaying STM memories to the LTM.

Title: Session-Bound Behavioral Watermarking for Autonomous LLM Agents

  • Authors: Md Facklasur Rahaman, Yong Xiang, Md Palash Uddin, Shang Gao, Guang Hua, Leo Yu Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38579
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38579
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38579]] Session-Bound Behavioral Watermarking for Autonomous LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38579)
  • Keywords: watermark, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly acting through Web interfaces and external tools. They produce trajectories. Those trajectories may pass through several intermediaries. They can store, modify, or dispute those trajectories. In Web-based agent deployments, trajectories may pass through APIs, marketplaces, and tool servers. As such, a copied or disputed log may not by itself reveal which agent owner produced it. Behavioral watermarking can embed ownership evidence in agent action choices. However, existing methods mainly address detection, removal, rewriting, or imitation. They do not directly test whether repeated observation of watermarked trajectories enables cross-session forgery by exploiting learned action preferences. We formulate this learned-forgery threat and propose AgentAnchor, a session-keyed behavioral watermark for owner attribution from trajectory logs. We derive each step key from a secret key, a fresh public session identifier, and semantic states of recent observations, while a guarded digest of the action-policy state diversifies linear coding rows. It embeds parity evidence through a KL-bounded exponential tilt, and a trusted verifier checks a disputed log without rerunning the agent, using wrong-key calibration and abstention.

Title: RoSAMDepth: Robust Self-supervised Depth Estimation Leveraging Segment Anything Model

  • Authors: Xuanang Gao, Zhiwei Ning, Gengming Zhang, Jiaxi Cao, Runze Yang, Zhonglong Zheng, Jie Yang, Rong Xiao, Wei Liu

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Robust depth estimation aims to maintain high-quality depths across diverse conditions. However, most existing methods estimate depth without taking into account the object-level information. As a result, the predicted depth may easily deviate within objects and become blurred under adverse conditions. To overcome this weakness, we propose RoSAMDepth, a novel framework that can assist robust self-supervised depth estimation in leveraging rich and diverse object-level priors from the Segment Anything Model (SAM). Extensive experiments across multiple datasets and diverse weather conditions demonstrate that our method produces sharper, more accurate depth predictions, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art methods. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: SpectralTF-MAE: A Pre-trained Spectral Foundation Model for Tokamak Fusion Plasma Diagnostics

  • Authors: Xiao Wang, Zhijie Lu, Duoqing Yang, Jiangtao Yu, Jin Tang, Zichao Lin, Hongming Zhang, Guosheng Xu, Bo Lyu, Qiang Liu

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Spectroscopic diagnostics provide rich information for characterizing plasma states in tokamak fusion experiments, while existing learning-based approaches are often designed for individual tasks and rely heavily on labeled data. In this work, we propose SpectralTF-MAE, a self-supervised spectral foundation model for tokamak plasma diagnostics. The model is pretrained on paired charge-exchange recombination spectroscopy (CXRS) and X-ray crystal spectroscopy (XCS) measurements through masked spectral reconstruction. To better capture diagnostically important spectral structures, we introduce a peak-aware masking strategy together with a peak-enhanced reconstruction objective, encouraging the model to focus on characteristic spectral regions. A shared Transformer encoder is employed to learn transferable representations across different diagnostic modalities. Experiments on EAST diagnostic data demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance across different tasks and consistently benefits from self-supervised spectral pretraining. These results indicate the potential of SpectralTF-MAE as a general and transferable representation learning framework for plasma spectroscopic diagnostics.

Title: Rethinking LLM Jailbreak Evaluation: Continuous Risk Landscapes Beyond Binary Outcomes

  • Authors: Yukai Zhou, Wenjie Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38595
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38595
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38595]] Rethinking LLM Jailbreak Evaluation: Continuous Risk Landscapes Beyond Binary Outcomes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38595)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, generative
  • Abstract: A binary success indicator is a natural representation for discriminative tasks such as image classification under adversarial perturbations, where each input is mapped to a single prediction. However, generative tasks are fundamentally different: an LLM jailbreak prompt induces a distribution over open-ended trajectories, while a sampled response reveals only one path. Yet LLM jailbreak evaluation typically retains the binary response indicator underlying attack success rate, obscuring the surrounding structure of possible generations. We introduce a continuous, context-conditioned risk landscape over first-token branches, estimated by fixing candidate openings and repeatedly sampling their continuations. This continuous risk-landscape perspective reveals two complementary properties: current decoder exposure and safe rerouting capacity, which have been obscured by binary-indicator-based ASR. The resulting representation also provides a common basis for comparing clean prompts, attacked variants, and attack strength against a known adaptive defense. Across multiple target models and jailbreak attacks, we uncover risky branches beneath apparently safe outputs, identify qualitatively different ways attacks reshape the model's opening distribution, and show that attacks strongest before a defense do not necessarily remain the strongest after the adaptive defense reacts. These findings recast jailbreaks as deformations of the surrounding continuation-risk landscape, establishing a more informative foundation for understanding jailbreak behavior and developing safer language models.

Title: Two-Stage Clipping: Linear-Time Approximate and Exact Mixed-Norm Projections for Transformers

  • Authors: Michel Barlaud, Jean-Paul Marmorat
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38598
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38598
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38598]] Two-Stage Clipping: Linear-Time Approximate and Exact Mixed-Norm Projections for Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38598)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) based on Transformer architectures have achieved remarkable performance across artificial intelligence tasks, but their computational cost remains a major bottleneck. Structured sparsification provides a promising approach to reducing this cost. In particular, $\ell_{1,\infty}$ projections can induce column-wise sparsity in model matrices, but existing proximal methods (Prox) typically require $\mathcal{O}(nm\log(nm))$ operations for matrices in $\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}$, limiting their computational and energy efficiency. In this paper, we introduce Two-Stage Clipping (TSC), an efficient structured sparsification operator inspired by bilevel optimization. Rather than solving a bilevel optimization problem in the strict sense, TSC decomposes the computation into two successive stages. First, group-level quantities are projected to allocate the global sparsity budget and identify active groups. Second, the resulting values are used as group-wise clipping thresholds for the matrix coefficients. For square attention matrices ($n \times n$), the complexity of our TSC method is $O(n^2)$, which is linear in the number of matrix coefficients. We establish a theoretical bound on the discrepancy between TSC and the exact proximal projection and show on synthetic data that the additional Euclidean approximation cost remains small in practice. Extensive benchmarks demonstrate substantial runtime improvements over proximal baselines and validate the theoretical norm identities underlying the proposed construction.

Title: STAR: Scalable TEE-based Anonymous Rate-limiter

  • Authors: Kosei Akama, Yoshimichi Nakatsuka, Koichi Moriyama, Keisuke Uehara
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38602
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38602
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38602]] STAR: Scalable TEE-based Anonymous Rate-limiter(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38602)
  • Keywords: secure, privacy, protect, attack
  • Abstract: Protecting Internet services from attacks such as TLS/HTTP floods requires rate limiting, yet identifying users to enforce these limits can compromise their privacy. Anonymous rate limiting promises to reconcile these goals, but existing schemes either struggle to scale or assume that services and authorities do not collude. We present STAR, which uses server-side trusted execution environments (TEEs) to overcome these limitations. By enabling lightweight cryptographic operations, TEEs make authentication efficient, but introduce a privacy challenge: malicious services can observe memory-access patterns to infer user secrets. We address this threat through countermeasures against secret-dependent memory accesses and assess our implementation using dynamic analysis. To extend these efficiency gains across servers, we also design a secure sharding mechanism that distributes client requests. We demonstrate that strong privacy guarantees can coexist with efficient authentication at scale; STAR processes authentication requests in tens of microseconds and handles approximately 13.9K requests per second per CPU core.

Title: V-CAPE: Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing

  • Authors: Iaroslav Melekhov, Roberto Alcover Couso, Francesco Taioli, Daniel Coelho, Artur Bekasov, Virginia Fernandez Arguedas, Jose Miguel Grande Saiz
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38611
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38611
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38611]] V-CAPE: Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38611)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Recent advances in generative image editing enable complex transformations such as background replacement, object manipulation, and pose adjustment from natural language instructions. However, verifying that these transformations preserve visual consistency, defined as the preservation of an object's intrinsic visual attributes, including geometry, texture, color, and fine-grained details, remains a largely unsolved problem. This challenge is particularly pronounced at scale, where manual inspection is infeasible and automated quality assessment (AutoQA) could become essential. In this work, we introduce Visual Consistency Assessment for Product Image Editing (V-CAPE), a large-scale dataset for evaluating visual consistency in generative image editing. The dataset consists of 40K unique source-target product image pairs, each annotated with binary accept/reject labels and structured failure modes capturing fine-grained inconsistencies such as geometric distortions, pose errors, and identity mismatches. V-CAPE combines real-world edited images with a synthetic subset constructed from 3D assets, enabling both realistic evaluation and controlled analysis. Vision-language models (VLMs) across all evaluated size categories on AutoQA achieve accuracy below 65%, indicating that the dataset poses a substantial challenge regardless of model scale. Our results highlight fundamental limitations of current models in verifying visual consistency under transformation, positioning V-CAPE as a benchmark for advancing fine-grained image editing evaluation.

Title: How Deterministic SAST and AI Security Agents Divide the Work: Coverage, Harness Policy and Cost in Snyk VulnBench 2.0

  • Authors: Liran Tal, Chris Liu, Charlotte Fereday, Sebastian Roth, Brian Clark

Title: Logical Guidance of Stochastic Interpolants

  • Authors: Gerrit Gerhartz, Francesco Alesiani, Jonathan Warrell, Henrik Christiansen, Matheus Ferraz, Mathias Niepert
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38648

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Guidance and composition of generative models have applications in visual design, chemistry, and biology. Logical guidance composes conditional scores exactly for diffusion models. We show that negation and exclusive disjunction admit exact conditional velocity composition on any interpolant, whereas conjunction, and therefore any formula containing one, is exact on Gaussian paths but in general incurs a drift-interaction error, so composed velocities need not sample the logical target.   • Abstract: Streaming federated learning (SFL) allows clients to train a shared model from continuously arriving data without retaining large local datasets. Title: Adversarial Self-Distillation

  • Authors: Mohammad Sadegh Akhondzadeh, Soroush H. Zargarbashi, Aleksandar Bojchevski
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38732
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38732
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38732]] Adversarial Self-Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38732)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Even though current safety-aligned language models are able to refuse harmful requests, their guard can still be bypassed by adversarial perturbations. As in other domains, adversarial training is considered a standard defense. However, due to the autoregressive nature of language generation, supervised finetuning on template refusals does not necessarily teach the model to refuse. Evidence of this is that the adversarially trained model still complies when the attacker injects a compliant prefill. We propose Adversarial Self-Distillation (ASD), which extends a model's own safe behavior to the space of adversarial perturbations. The teacher (the model itself given the clean prompt) can refuse the harmful request by default, or with the help of an additional instruction in its context. ASD distills this knowledge to the student (which is the same model under attack) at the prefixes that the student visits (due to on-policy sampling). These potentially include compliant openings. ASD achieves lower attack success than state-of-the-art defenses with little loss in multiple-choice accuracy, and it becomes able to recover a refusal even after a compliant opening.

Building on this framework, we introduce Rennala NSGD, a semi-asynchronous distributed algorithm, and establish wall-clock time complexity guarantees under fixed, heterogeneous worker computation times.   • Abstract: Differential privacy (DP) has become the standard for privacy-preserving data analysis, yet its practical implementation in federated or distributed settings often faces a significant utility gap compared to the central model. Our first contribution is to show that this is inherent in the aggregation model: any private selection algorithm relying on the standard noisy aggregation primitive requires $\Omega(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)$ samples. Furthermore, we show that this can be done using a near-optimal expected number $O(d\log^*d)$ of SVT queries. Together with existing lower bounds for the shuffle model, this result establishes an exponential separation between the SVT model and shuffle or aggregation-based models. We demonstrate the model's broad applicability to diverse tasks such as $F_p$ loss minimization, heavy-hitter identification, and sparse mean estimation.

Title: CARI: Cross-Boundary Adversarial Runtime Injection in Coding-Agent Workflows

  • Authors: Jie Fu, Shuang Wu, Ashish Kundu, Wendy Hui Wang, Yuan Hong
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38799
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38799
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38799]] CARI: Cross-Boundary Adversarial Runtime Injection in Coding-Agent Workflows(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38799)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Coding agents increasingly develop, test, and integrate software whose components are owned by different developers, teams, or services. This exposes a distinct attack surface: a malicious or compromised peer development role can manipulate a live component of the software under development (e.g., a backend service, test harness, CI job, or deployment service) and influence the runtime feedback that another role's coding agent must process to complete its task. Although such a component has legitimate diagnostic authority to report its own state, it has no action authority over the victim's local files, credentials, shell, or development environment. We introduce Cross-Boundary Adversarial Runtime Injection (CARI), a direct runtime-feedback injection setting that exploits this authority mismatch. Rather than explicitly compromising the victim's prompt, model, tools, or local environment, the attacker covertly embeds malicious instructions in task-relevant runtime feedback produced by a peer-controlled development component. The victim then interprets that feedback as part of normal debugging or recovery, and may translate the diagnostic guidance into unauthorized victim-side actions. We instantiate ten different attacks based on CARI, spanning resource exhaustion, information exposure, and development-state manipulation. Across four programming languages and five code-capable models, CARI succeeds in 2,845/3,000 (94.8%) trials. Furthermore, CARI transfers effectively to product-level coding agents, achieving 61.3% attack success on Codex and 58.7% on Claude Code. By contrast, prompt-level defenses exhibit a security-utility trade-off: generic warnings are ineffective, whereas explicit policies suppress attacks but substantially over-refuse legitimate actions.

  • Authors: Wenyu Du, Stephen Chung
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38822

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers increase computational capacity through the repeated application of shared blocks, but their recurrent depth makes conventional full-depth decoding expensive. While existing speculative pipelines accelerate generation by processing consecutive token positions at varying depths, they are typically limited by sequential decoding overheads. We introduce CopyLoop, a training-free decoding algorithm that combines low-cost context-copy proposals with shallow screening and continuous recurrent scheduling. Rather than step-by-step generation, CopyLoop proposes multi-token suffixes from repeated context. We evaluate CopyLoop across six model checkpoints from the Ouro, Raven, and Huginn families, covering code generation and multi-step mathematical reasoning. Furthermore, ablation studies confirm a strong synergy between shallow screening and continuous scheduling, while our analyses show that CopyLoop requires only lightweight model calibration and yields greater speedups on longer generations with reusable contexts.

We propose Local Point Tracking (LPT), a robust framework that combines a single-frame detector, local point propagation, and stateless mask refinement. LPT follows a localize first, snap second pipeline. First, a single-frame semantic detector generates sparse anchors, within which LPT initializes local query points (SAM-supported by default, with a regular grid as fallback). A point-propagation module tracks these points through detector-missed frames and recovers temporal bounding boxes from their geometry. Second, because point-derived boxes can be loose around the visible boundary, a box-snap step refines the selected box to the target boundary. This decomposition keeps temporal state in local point coordinates rather than a stateful mask or whole-region representation, while the spatial refinement module handles single-frame boundary precision without feeding mask errors back into temporal memory. Extensive evaluations on two distinct datasets demonstrate that our proposed LPT method achieves strong overall performance against existing baselines, with the best mAP_50, success AUC, and missing rate on both datasets. Furthermore, ablation studies confirm that point tracking primarily enhances temporal tracking robustness, while box-snap refinement effectively improves boundary tightness.

Title: What to randomize matters: a controlled study of volume, axis and representation in low-data molecular generation

  • Authors: Neerav Kaushal, Alisa Teige, Ajay MNV Penmatsa
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CE, cs.CL, q-bio.BM, q-bio.QM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38835
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38835
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38835]] What to randomize matters: a controlled study of volume, axis and representation in low-data molecular generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38835)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Chemical language models can struggle to generate new molecules when trained on small datasets, where a model may reproduce training examples rather than learn a useful generative distribution. Randomized SMILES augmentation varies atom traversal, whereas Sequential Attachment-based Fragment Embedding (SAFE) also allows the order of fragment blocks to vary. How these distinct sources of representational variation affect low-data molecular generation has not been clearly studied. Here, using 1,000 seed molecules to train an autoregressive sequence model, we compare canonical SAFE with randomized SAFE at increasing corpus sizes, isolate atom-order and fragment-order axes of SAFE randomization, and compare fully randomized SAFE with randomized SMILES at molecule-by-molecule matched training corpora. Three findings emerge. First, randomization of SAFE alone raises the genuine yield of novel molecules more than 450-fold, turning a model that merely reproduces its training set into a practical molecular generator. Second, the two axes of randomization act jointly rather than additively: atom order alone reaches a tenth, and fragment order alone a seventh, of the genuine yield obtained when both vary, which is four times their combined contribution. Third, at matched corpus size and compute, randomized SAFE delivers 2.8 times the genuine yield of randomized SMILES. Welch tests with Games-Howell post-hoc comparisons resolve every difference on the primary outcomes. In the low-data regime, thus, what one randomizes rather than only how much determines whether augmentation produces new molecules or merely new spellings of the training set.

Title: Discovery of Diverse and Realistic Financial Tail-Risk Using Generative Flow Networks

  • Authors: Adir Serruya, Gilad Katz
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38838
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38838

  • Keywords: robust, generative
  • Abstract: Accurate modeling of rare, high-impact financial events is essential for robust risk management. Sampling-based techniques such as Sequential Markov Chain Monte Carlo (SMCMC) generate diverse scenarios but inefficiently explore high-risk regions, while Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) converges on narrow sets of scenarios, limiting diversity. We present GRID, which uses Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) -- samplers that draw objects in proportion to a reward -- to systematically generate diverse and realistic financial tail-risk scenarios. Building on continuous GFlowNets, GRID introduces a flexible mixture-policy architecture with variable-specific distribution families for high-dimensional, sequential macro-financial data, together with plausibility constraints that keep generated trajectories realistic. A central practical advantage is robustness to reward-proxy imperfection: because GFlowNets sample proportionally rather than maximize, they remain broadly exploratory even when the oracle's out-of-sample accuracy is low -- an unavoidable reality in financial forecasting. On three real-world crises, GRID improves scenario diversity, realism, and tail-risk coverage over RL and MCMC baselines, and ablations over oracle architectures and distribution families yield practical insights into continuous GFlowNet behavior under noisy rewards.

  • Abstract: Anomaly-based network intrusion detection systems (NIDS) can detect previously unseen attacks by identifying deviations from benign traffic.   • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38895

  • Keywords: security, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Typed decision models are an emerging class of language models that make decisions instead of generating free-text responses. They answer in a single fast and cheap query and are increasingly used, including in security-sensitive tasks. However, red teaming of these models leaves two gaps: existing adaptive attacks do not use their speed, and few studies have searched for universal adversarial examples. We fill both gaps on Jev, the representative typed decision model, with a quick black-box query-based attack that calls no attacker language model. We conduct extensive experiments on 12 datasets with different data types and application scenarios. Title: Interventional Training Reduces Causal Bypass in In-Context Introspection

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38911
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38911
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38911]] Interventional Training Reduces Causal Bypass in In-Context Introspection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38911)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Introspection, the ability of a system to access and reason about its internal signals, is crucial for language model (LM) self-consistency and scalable interpretability. Prior work argues that LMs possess introspective capabilities, and that training (SFT) could enhance such behavior. However, most setups leave open the possibility of "causal bypass," in which the self-report relies on signals that are correlated with the self-report's subject, while being causally unrelated to it. Although high-level concepts are difficult to intervene on, lower-level signals (such as a single neuron activation) allow precise interventions. Understanding such signals can serve as a minimal prerequisite before understanding more complex introspection. The mechanism generalizes across token positions and scales with more data. Our results demonstrate that while causally grounded introspection is possible, it is not guaranteed -- emphasizing the need to train on mechanisms, rather than behaviors alone.

Title: Rethinking GFlowNet Training: Negative Reinforce Dynamics

  • Authors: Pedro Dall'Antonia, Daniel Csillag, Diego Mesquita
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38994
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38994
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.38994]] Rethinking GFlowNet Training: Negative Reinforce Dynamics(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38994)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are models that learn to sample discrete objects proportional to a target reward. They are trained by minimizing balance errors over sampled trajectories, which inherently ties correctness to exploration: if a region is not visited, balance cannot be enforced there. Adaptive exploration is therefore crucial, yet standard training relies on a semi-gradient update that treats the sampling distribution as fixed at each optimization step. On the other hand, the full gradient reveals a second mechanism that reduces the observed errors in an anti-exploratory manner. In this paper, we establish a connection between the full gradient and min-min dynamics between the GFlowNet and a decoupled sampler, showing that the sampler reduces observed violations by avoiding high-error regions. This insight motivates a bridge between two-player games and single-policy dynamics, which underpins the design of our proposed method, Negative Reinforce Dynamics (NRD). By tying the parameters of an adversarial game together, we recover a single-policy dynamic that extends exploration while maintaining correctness. In standard benchmarks, our method outperforms existing approaches in both convergence speed and exploration efficiency, without training a separate teacher or auxiliary exploration policy.

There are several feature scorers to choose from, and often no single metric or ground truth is available to guarantee the quality of their results. In this scenario, visualization can become valuable to support the decision of which method to choose and how good its results are. For this goal, this work presents "weighted t-SNE." It modifies the relationship between data points in the embedded 2D space to reflect the importance of each dimension of the original datasets as assessed by a feature scorer. This research discusses how to implement weighted t-SNE, proposes the silhouette coefficient as a numerical evaluation of the results, and shows several examples of its use in practice. Synthetic and real-world tabular datasets are used in the experiments together with nine feature scorers, ranging from Mutual Information to neural networks. Each feature scorer produces unique visualizations, and weighted t-SNE can be used to compare and choose the one that better suits a given dataset and task. Weighted t-SNE can also visually show the importance of features learned by machine learning models and help us see how they are organizing the data, increasing their interpretability.

Title: The Search for Statistical Indistinguishability Between Real and Fake Images

  • Authors: Mohaimin Rafi, Otavio Moraes Rosa, Anshuman Chhabra, Utkarsh Ojha
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39014
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39014
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39014]] The Search for Statistical Indistinguishability Between Real and Fake Images(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39014)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Images produced by generative models appear strikingly similar to real ones. In this work, we study whether perfection has been reached by generated content in terms of matching the properties of real data. We train deep neural networks to separate real data from fake and observe their test accuracies. Only a chance performance has any scope of proving statistical indistinguishability. However, since generative models are known to sometimes introduce small errors, e.g., low level checkerboard artifacts, we create progressive abstractions of the original real/fake images to potentially bring them to a space where those artifacts get wiped away. These include operations such as blurring, downsizing, cropping, converting RGB images into edge/semantic maps, etc. To our surprise, almost none of the abstractions are able to make fake data indistinguishable from real. The only cases when neural network based classifiers fail to separate the two categories is when the data is abstracted to such an extent that the original content seems mostly lost to the human eye, e.g., downsizing a $256 \times 256$ RGB image of human face to $4 \times 4$ resolution. We observe this trend across four different datasets of varying complexity (MNIST, CelebA, FFHQ and GenImage) and across various types of generative models (GANs, VAEs, diffusion models). This shows that the nature of fake artifacts extends all the way from low level to very high level in images. The simple implication is that fake images are much more different from real than we are used to think.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39020
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39020
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39020]] Distilling the Shape of Uncertainty: What On-Policy Distillation Learns and How to Predict It(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39020)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is increasingly adopted in the post-training of large language models. However, a stronger teacher may fail to improve the student. Prior work attributes this to a lack of new knowledge, but what that knowledge is remains unclear. We show that OPD learns not only \textit{how to branch}, i.e., which candidate tokens to consider, but also \textit{where to branch}, i.e., where to keep reasoning paths open. The existing token-overlap metric captures only the former. We introduce the entropy shape, the sequence of next-token entropies, to characterize the latter, and propose the Entropy Shape Correlation (ESC) to measure student-teacher agreement in entropy shape. Tracking ESC throughout training and intervening on a teacher's entropy while preserving its token preferences, we empirically show that \textit{where to branch} causally drives OPD gains, independently of \textit{how to branch}. Finally, we formulate OPD outcome prediction as a teacher selection task and establish an evaluation protocol for it, under which pre-OPD ESC selects the best teacher for both students and outperforms existing predictors. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: FairSplit: Decomposing the Embedding Space for Fair Classification

  • Authors: Andrii Shkabrii, Anna Beer, Ylli Sadikaj, Claudia Plant
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39031
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39031
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39031]] FairSplit: Decomposing the Embedding Space for Fair Classification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39031)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Fair classification faces a twofold challenge: maintaining high classification accuracy while limiting reliance on sensitive information. Our method FairSplit achieves this by learning a decomposition of the embedding space into a Fair Space used for prediction and a Sensitive Space that isolates sensitive information. During training, the model must simultaneously learn the classification task and separate fair from sensitive information. The Sensitive Space gives sensitive attributes a destination, so the Fair Space can be used for prediction without relying on them. This decomposition is guided via a learnable mask and the Hilbert-Schmidt Independence Criterion (HSIC) as a measure of statistical dependence. We provide theoretical guarantees showing that the demographic parity gap is upper-bounded by both (i) the learned dimensionality of the Fair Space and (ii) the HSIC between the Fair Space and sensitive attributes. FairSplit effectively handles multiclass tasks and non-binary sensitive attributes, overcoming key limitations of existing fair classification methods. Experiments on established fairness benchmarks show that FairSplit achieves competitive accuracy-fairness trade-offs. Code is available at this https URL.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39056
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39056]] When Does Closed-Loop Curriculum Adaptation Help GRPO? An Analysis on Countdown(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39056)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement fine-tuning has become an effective post-training paradigm for improving the reasoning ability of large language models, but it remains sample-inefficient when the training distribution is mismatched to the model's current capability. In group-relative objectives such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), problems that are too hard often produce uniformly failed rollouts, while problems that are too easy provide little useful advantage signal. In this work, we introduce CLOSEDLOOPGAUSSIAN, a lightweight adaptive curriculum method for efficient reinforcement fine-tuning on ordered reasoning tasks. The controller accelerates training when the model is ahead of a reference curriculum, slows down when the model falls behind, and broadens sampling when earlier difficulties regress. This feedback mechanism keeps training focused on tasks that are challenging but still learnable, without changing the reward function, model architecture, or underlying GRPO optimizer. Experiments on symbolic arithmetic reasoning task show that CLOSEDLOOPGAUSSIAN improves exact-match accuracy over vanilla GRPO and fixed open-loop curricula, with the strongest gains in intermediate-difficulty regimes where group-relative learning signals are most informative. Title: HEARSAYBENCH: Can AI Navigate from Abstract Human Rights to Lived Lives?

  • Authors: Ava Iranmanesh, Sobhan Lotfi, Ali Iranmanesh, Liwei Jiang
  • Subjects: cs.CL

  • Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) reach users worldwide, personal counseling has emerged as one of their most consequential applications, sometimes in life-critical situations. Title: Closed Gates Leak: Decomposing Adjacent-Layer Cancellation in Gated Language Models

  • Authors: Harvey Dam, Ganesh Gopalakrishnan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39068
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39068
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39068]] Closed Gates Leak: Decomposing Adjacent-Layer Cancellation in Gated Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39068)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: In transformer language models with gated multilayer perceptrons (MLPs), adjacent layers write partly against each other into the residual stream. Previous work interpreted this cancellation as layers proposing features and the next layer selectively rejecting the inappropriate ones. We decompose the cancellation by removing the previous layer's write from the input of the next MLP: what the next layer writes along that write splits into what it writes anyway and how much less its MLP writes because the write is present, which we call the damping, and we trace the damping to the neurons that carry it. In gated MLPs the damping is carried by neurons whose gates are closed. SiLU and GELU are slightly negative below zero, so a closed gate still passes a small negative multiple of what the neuron reads, and a neuron whose output weight points along its input weight then subtracts a fraction of the direction it read. Summed over such neurons, closed gates contract the average direction a gated MLP reads, and the previous layer's write is not singled out: the same layer's write at another token is damped as much or more, and the eigenvalue signature from which that work concluded that the correction is selective appears for another token's write as well. Over training the damping grows while what the next layer writes anyway stays flat, and cancellation begins when the damping overtakes it; annealing the negative side of the gate activation away in continued pretraining removes the damping and the cancellation. Adjacent-layer cancellation is therefore weak evidence about which features a model rejects: it is produced by a contraction that gated MLPs apply, on average over directions, to whatever they read.

  • Abstract: Triple set prediction (TSP) is to recover missing relational triples in a knowledge graph by predicting the triple set instead of isolated links.   • Authors: Zerui Wang

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39147
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39147

  • Abstract: 4D perception models, which classify 3D point-cloud videos, are being built into robots, assistive monitoring and AR, yet before deploying one a practitioner cannot check which cue it relies on: how a body part moves, or what shape it has. 3D attribution is single-frame, video attribution is confined to RGB pixels, gradient maps need white-box access, attention is absent from state-space backbones, and point-level perturbations assume a point correspondence across frames that real depth and LiDAR data lack. We validate KCA on the official PSTNet, P4Transformer and Mamba4D trained on MSR-Action3D. The decisive test is an audit: two binary tasks are built from real clips whose classes differ only in the motion or only in the shape of the same arm, and the motion share of the arm's importance follows the defining cue on all three backbones (0.83 vs. 0.60 on P4Transformer, 0.86 vs. 0.30 on PSTNet, 0.92 vs. 0.61 on Mamba4D), a distinction no single attribution map can make. The split costs no faithfulness: the necessity maps have the lowest deletion AUC on P4Transformer and PSTNet, tie with the best baseline on Mamba4D, and lose their correlation with the trained model's once the weights are randomised. Skeleton ground truth, re-sampling stability and cost complete the validation.

  • Abstract: With the rapid advancement of text-to-image diffusion models, increasingly realistic AI-generated content has raised serious concerns about misuse and copyright infringement. Furthermore, we introduce a decoder-guided adaptive injection mechanism that uses decoding confidence as feedback to dynamically calibrate watermark strength, reinforcing weak watermark signals and improving robustness under image transformations. Against six classical and learned non-rigid registration baselines on held-out identities, our method leads the strongest ones by 3-4 times on per-vertex error and baselines that collapse the head onto the visible region by more than an order of magnitude, and is simultaneously the most accurate and fastest method on ground-truth-free scans from two independent real-scan datasets, NoW and NPHM. Title: Provably Secure Diffusion Watermarking Against Removal Adversaries with Batched Detector Access

  • Authors: De Zhang Lee, Ee-Chien Chang
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39189
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39189

  • Abstract: Recent latent-based watermarking methods can embed undetectable watermarks into AI-generated content. We show that such defenses are insecure under a stronger threat model motivated by publicly reachable detectors: an adversary with batched detector access can reconstruct the transformations, permanently re-exposing the decision boundary. To address this, we propose a watermarking scheme based on a hybrid of long-term and one-time secret transformations, which prevents the reuse of structure across samples and keeps the long-term secret hidden. Besides undetectability, we show that this scheme is provably secure against removal adversaries with batched detector access, in the sense that no probabilistic polynomial-time adversary can achieve a non-negligible advantage over an adversary restricted to adding isotropic white-noise with a $\sqrt{2}\times$ larger perturbation budget, and we show this $\sqrt{2}$ factor to be tight. Evaluations on Stable Diffusion 2.1 and XL demonstrate the attack against the existing defense, and show that our scheme preserves generation quality and withstands common perturbations and targeted removal attacks.

Title: Analyzing Condensation on Patterned Surfaces via Deep Learning

Hierarchical tiling with per-tile inference followed by post-processing extended SAM3's detection cap and raised the effective resolution of small droplets. We created two datasets: High Quality Droplet Dataset (N=365,826; HQDD) with homogeneous surfaces and Low Quality Droplet Dataset (N=63,577; LQDD) with patterned, defect-laden surfaces under adverse lighting. TileSAM3 closely reproduced thresholding annotations on HQDD, surpassed it over sixfold on LQDD (Average Precision 0.57 versus 0.088), and outperformed other deep learning models on all metrics evaluated. Combined with KD-tree tracking, TileSAM3 identified localized nucleation and coalescence in narrow active bands on a stiffness-patterned surface. TileSAM3 enables analysis of understudied inhomogeneous substrates with poor lighting and videography which facilitates faster, lower cost research of condensation materials.

  • Abstract: Accurate time series forecasting (TSF) is essential for Web applications, such as traffic monitoring and online resource management. Our empirical analysis reveals that TPI is prevalent across widely used TSF benchmarks, with substantially higher forecasting errors on less frequent patterns. Furthermore, Routing-Guided Semantic Alignment (RGSA) transfers the pattern-specific knowledge learned by the router to cross-modal alignment through mutual information maximization, preserving distinctive temporal information across different patterns. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of IMRouter, particularly in improving forecasting performance on less frequent temporal patterns.

  • Abstract: The decoding order of masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can substantially affect generation quality, yet common decoders typically apply the same reveal rule throughout generation. Confidence provides a strong default, but it measures how decisively a position can be predicted now, not how useful revealing that position will be for subsequent decoding. We propose Cord, a lightweight prompt-conditioned policy that retains confidence decoding as a stable reference and learns when and how to depart from it through geometric residuals over generation direction, proximity to revealed context, and two-sided infilling. The residuals adapt to both the prompt and the evolving partial response. Rather than sampling a new policy action at every decoding step, Cord samples a single low-dimensional coefficient matrix per rollout, which induces an entire state-dependent reveal trajectory. This yields a contextual-bandit formulation trained directly from sequence-level rewards, without step-level credit assignment or backbone updates. Across three dLLMs and five reasoning, code, and constraint-satisfaction benchmarks, Cord improves average task success from 32.0% under confidence decoding to 48.2%, and further improves over the best per-domain fixed residual. Behavioral audits show that the learned residuals induce substantial, task-dependent changes in reveal locality and two-sided infilling. Cord adds only $0.53$M trainable parameters, requires no additional backbone forward passes, and incurs only 5.39% inference latency overhead.

  • Abstract: Although decades of research have yielded sophisticated mechanisms for enforcing memory safety, speculative execution can subvert these protections through transient memory operations. A naive combination of defenses from both domains raises two fundamental challenges: (1) which combination of otherwise fragmented defenses can ensure dual memory safety under speculative and non-speculative executions? We present DualGuard, a unified framework for dual memory protection under non-speculative and speculative executions. DualGuard systematically analyzes memory behaviors, classifying them into four categories, and further identifies the minimal composition requirements for dual memory safety, which allow us to strategically combine existing mitigations and design new ones to avoid redundant or excessive protections. We implement DualGuard through a combination of static analysis and runtime enforcement within the LLVM infrastructure. Our evaluation demonstrates that DualGuard reduces overhead by 3%-72% compared to naive compositions of fragmented defenses. In some cases, it even incurs lower overhead than approaches that guarantee memory safety solely under either non-speculative or speculative execution.

We evaluate SAM 3 under this setting and find that, despite its powerful positive-only few-shot segmentation capability, frozen SAM 3 does not consistently treat negative visual prompts as exclusion evidence. Title: LinQuant: Functional Invariances for Low-Precision Linear Recurrences

  • Authors: Neehal Tumma, Philipp Nazari, Rohan Cherukuri, Daniela Rus, T. Konstantin Rusch
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39418
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39418
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.39418]] LinQuant: Functional Invariances for Low-Precision Linear Recurrences(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39418)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Linear recurrences are emerging as practical alternatives to softmax attention in large language models, appearing in both standalone and hybrid architectures. However, despite their rapid emergence, low-precision inference remains much less developed for these models than for softmax attention. During prefill, chunkwise-parallel algorithms cast much of the recurrence as dense matrix multiplications, suggesting a natural path to low-precision execution. Simply quantizing these operations in their native form, however, can be fragile. In this work, we identify functional invariances that preserve the full-precision computation while making the operands more amenable to quantization. Building on this observation, we introduce LinQuant, a framework for low-precision recurrent prefill based on seven such invariances, including rotations, diagonal rescalings, and alternative placements of recurrent decay and write factors. We derive when these transformations remain valid across common recurrence families spanning linear, gated, and delta-rule updates, and show that they provide complementary degrees of freedom for improving low-precision accuracy. Finally, we translate this framework to production by augmenting SSDi8, an existing low-precision prefill pipeline for Mamba-2, with the applicable invariances. LinQuant improves perplexity at matched latency on Mamba-2 1.3B, 2.7B, and 8B and Nemotron-H 8B, demonstrating the practical utility of invariance-based quantization for recurrent models.

  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models can recognize visual content yet struggle to reason reliably over it across multiple steps.
