Yet, this increase in performance comes at the cost of decreased interpretability, as the semantic meaning of Earth embeddings is unknown. To address this challenge, we borrow methods from the field of computational neuroscience, which aim to interpret activity in (biological) neurons. Here, we adapt and apply these methods to assign meaning to AlphaEarth embeddings. We analyse embedding features both as stand-alone data, equivalent to spontaneous neural activity, and in relation to land cover and elevation correlates, equivalent to stimulus-response neural activity. Together, our analyses start to unravel what these Earth embeddings represent, which is critical for developing trustworthy downstream applications.

Title: Textual Response-Aware Guidance for Zero-Shot Adversarial Robustness of Vision-Language Models

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40309

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40309]] Textual Response-Aware Guidance for Zero-Shot Adversarial Robustness of Vision-Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40309)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit strong zero-shot generalization but remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks. Existing adversarial fine-tuning methods often use pre-trained model guidance to preserve image--text alignment while improving robustness. Our analysis of visual representation drift reveals complementary limitations of two representative forms of guidance. Matching visual features provides broad coverage by constraining all directions equally, but lacks semantic emphasis. Matching prediction distributions prioritizes semantically relevant directions, but remains limited by the candidate texts and prediction probabilities. These limitations can hinder preservation of pre-trained image--text relationships and degrade zero-shot performance. To address these issues, we propose Textual Response-Aware Guidance (TRAG), which constrains visual representation drift using the pre-trained text encoder's responses to local variations in input embeddings. Specifically, TRAG constructs anchor-level constraints from the response directions and magnitudes around semantic anchors, then aggregates them using pre-trained image--anchor relevance, yielding an image-adaptive constraint. Extensive experiments across architectures and tasks demonstrate state-of-the-art clean and robust performance. Further analysis shows that TRAG helps preserve pre-trained image--text relationships.

Four System One models (Jev 1.13, Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1, Laya) and GPT-6 Luna (OpenAI Decisions API) were compared with GPT-6 Sol and two local generative models. The primary outcome was accuracy; secondary outcomes included calibration, discrimination, accuracy of answers with probability >=0.90 and language differences. Results: In RheumaMIR, Jev's accuracy was 93.0% in Spanish and 91.9% in English, below GPT-6 Sol (99.4%; Holm-adjusted P<=0.005); GPT-6 Luna reached 97.7%, Clef 91.9-93.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 86.0-87.8%, without detectable language differences. Jev's answers with probability >=0.90 (76-77% of answers) were 98.5% correct. In RheumBench, accuracy was lower: GPT-6 Sol 76.9%, GPT-6 Luna 63.5%, Clef 59.6%, Jev 50.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 40.4% (each System One model below GPT-6 Sol, Holm-adjusted P<=0.045; chance 9.6%). Jev became overconfident (mean probability 0.659; over/underconfidence index +0.159), and its answers with probability >=0.90 (25% of cases) were 84.6% correct, against 95.5% for GPT-6 Luna (42%). Laya performed near chance in both benchmarks. Conclusion: The best-performing System One models were highly accurate on examination questions but less accurate on diagnostic vignettes, remaining below GPT-6 Sol. Jev's high-confidence predictions were less reliable on vignettes, highlighting the need for task-specific evaluation and calibration.

Title: How to Loop Your Transformer: Loop Scaling, Specialization, and Parallel Inference

  • Authors: Chanakya Ekbote, Artidoro Pagnoni, Devendra Singh Sachan, Shruti Bhosale, Mike Lewis, Maryam Fazel-Zarandi, Shashank Rajput, Sujan Kumar Gonugondla
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40325
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40325
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40325]] How to Loop Your Transformer: Loop Scaling, Specialization, and Parallel Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40325)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looping in transformer models reuses weights by repeatedly applying the same transformer, or parts of it, making the model deeper without increasing its parameter count. Whether looping is useful depends on which constraint dominates: model size, training compute, or autoregressive inference efficiency. We study these trade-offs jointly in dense decoder-only transformers trained from scratch, varying which parts of the model are looped, how many loops are applied, and how information is passed between loops. Our experiments span models with up to 1B parameters, trained on up to 155B tokens with up to $1.07\times10^{21}$ FLOPs and 32 loops. We find that looping the full transformer generally gives the strongest quality at a fixed parameter budget, whereas looping a middle block of layers gives a better return when training FLOPs are constrained. Adding loops is not uniformly beneficial: gains eventually saturate, reverse, or become unstable. This motivates asking whether forcing every loop to use the same weights limits the benefit of deeper looping. We address this by adding small loop-specific low-rank adapters that improve quality, with larger gains as more loops are added. Looped transformers also suffer a decoding slowdown proportional to the number of loops. Parallel Loop Transformers address this by passing hidden states between loops across adjacent token positions, allowing loop stages to run concurrently. However, in our experiments the original formulation does not consistently retain the quality gains of ordinary looping. We find that preserving the current-token embedding and separately normalizing it and the previous-token hidden state before combining them recovers strong performance across model scales and training budgets. Together, these results show that effective looping requires matching the design to the resource constraint and inference setting.

Title: A Unified Hessian-Guided Framework for Post-Training Quantization

  • Authors: Mads Toftrup, Marco Federici, Boris van Breugel, Paul Whatmough, Markus Nagel
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40345
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40345
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40345]] A Unified Hessian-Guided Framework for Post-Training Quantization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40345)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Low-bit quantization enables efficient deployment of large language models by reducing memory footprint and computational cost. Achieving high accuracy at low precision requires selecting quantization ranges, linear transformations, and weight rounding decisions. Existing approaches typically optimize these components independently using local reconstruction objectives, which fail to account for the varying impact of quantization errors on downstream predictions. Direct optimization of task-level metrics would provide a more faithful objective, but is challenging due to the discontinuous nature of quantization operations and the high computational cost of evaluating their downstream effects. In this work, we derive a Hessian-guided approximation of the output change induced by joint weight and activation quantization. Based on a tractable Fisher approximation, we formulate a unified post-training quantization framework that enables the joint optimization of quantization ranges, linear transformations, and weight rounding under a common output-sensitivity objective. The resulting method remains practical for modern large language models and consistently outperforms state-of-the-art transformation and rounding methods across both channel-wise and block-wise quantization settings.

  • Abstract: Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. Title: Your LR Schedule May Not Be Principled: Effective Learning Rate Scheduling in Weight-Normalized Training

  • Authors: Qifan Yu, Xinyu Ma, Xingyan Bin, Di He
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40362
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40362
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40362]] Your LR Schedule May Not Be Principled: Effective Learning Rate Scheduling in Weight-Normalized Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40362)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Weight-normalized (WN) training is emerging as a compelling alternative to conventional weight decay (WD), with recent studies reporting substantial pretraining loss gains. We show that these gains are driven primarily by the induced effective learning rate (ELR) trajectory rather than by normalization alone. Matching ELR trajectories largely collapses the training loss dynamics of WN and WD. Schedule design should therefore target ELR rather than the nominal learning rate (LR) alone. Building on this principle, we propose AIR (Affine Inverse-square-Root decay), which combines a theoretically derived $1/\sqrt{t}$ decay with an affine adjustment to satisfy prescribed peak and terminal LRs over a finite training horizon. We further identify late-stage ELR scheduling as a key source of its gains, because a shared late schedule attenuates the effect of earlier LR choices. Experiments on 128 B-token Mixture-of-Experts pretraining demonstrate that AIR outperforms cosine and other candidate schedules in training loss, downstream performance, and hyperparameter robustness, reducing terminal training loss by approximately 0.01 relative to cosine at matched cumulative LR. Applying the same ELR trajectory to WD reproduces the corresponding training dynamics, showing that the benefits extend beyond fixed-norm training and establishing ELR as the principled scheduling object.

Title: Composing and Steering Diffusion Models with Generalized Feynman-Kac Correctors

  • Authors: Wael Mattar, Daniel Freedman
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40383
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40383
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40383]] Composing and Steering Diffusion Models with Generalized Feynman-Kac Correctors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40383)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion models have become the leading paradigm in generative modeling, yet many applications require sampling from distributions that are not those of any single pretrained model, but are instead defined through combinations of several models, possibly tilted toward high values of a downstream reward. Naively combining the scores of the models yields incorrect samples, while existing corrections are restricted to specific families of targets. In this work, we introduce the generalized Feynman-Kac correctors (GFKC), a principled framework for sampling from an arbitrary target function of several pretrained diffusion models. We show that the geometric interpolation between an arbitrary initial function and the target function of the models satisfies a Feynman--Kac partial differential equation, and derive its potential in closed form. To evaluate this potential during generation, we further generalize the log-probability ODE to track the density of each model along trajectories driven by a different velocity field. The resulting algorithm operates entirely in the log domain, incorporates reward-based steering at no additional conceptual cost, and corrects the generation on the fly, without retraining or fine-tuning the models. We validate the method on analytic Gaussian models, where it closely matches the ground-truth targets, and on diffusion models trained on MNIST, where it samples from non-trivial combinations of the models and enables effective steering.

Title: Understanding and Mitigating Role Spoofing in LLMs

  • Authors: Ezra Edelman, Benjamin Edelman, Surbhi Goel, Eran Malach

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40396

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Despite growing capabilities, LLMs remain susceptible to prompt injections: malicious inputs that can manipulate or hijack models. In particular, it has recently been observed that LLMs can exhibit role confusion (Ye et al., 2026), where models attribute the source of text based on its style instead of its labeled role. Malicious text in tool outputs (for instance, a malicious webpage accessed by an agent) can leverage role confusion to impersonate the user, an attack we call role spoofing}. In this work, we seek to understand and mitigate role spoofing. We study how models track "who said what" in a conversation, showing that they use cues from both content (e.g., semantics and writing style) and structure (e.g., control tokens and formatting). We show that LLMs are highly susceptible to role spoofing attacks that combine both fake structure and content. This can explain why frontier models, with increased resistance to many injection attacks, can still be susceptible to role spoofing. Finally, we propose a new method for detecting role spoofing using linear probes trained to predict roles in a conversation, and show that it is effective across different settings.

Title: Preserving Utility in Continual LLM Unlearning via Orthogonal Projections

  • Authors: Juan Belieni, Ana Carolina Erthal, Eliezer de Souza da Silva, Diego Mesquita
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40483
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40483
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40483]] Preserving Utility in Continual LLM Unlearning via Orthogonal Projections(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40483)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Unlearning concerns the task of removing the influence of a sensitive portion of the training data, such as protected, copyrighted or private text, from a model without retraining it from scratch. While unlearning requests happen continuously during a model's lifetime, the vast majority of LLM unlearning methods are ill-suited for iterated removal, yielding steep decay in forgetting and utility upon sequential unlearning. This decay stems from interference between successive updates, as each new deletion can undo earlier ones or push the model further along directions already degraded by them. To address this, we introduce Continual Orthogonal Projection (COP), a gradient-projection scheme that restricts each update to the orthogonal complement of activation subspaces cached from prior forget sets. This approach provably guarantees first-order non-interference with earlier deletions. COP stores only a low-rank basis per layer, requiring neither past gradients nor previous forget sets, and plugs into unlearning methods that update the model through gradient steps on a differentiable loss without modifying its objective. Experiments on standard LLM unlearning benchmarks (TOFU and RWKU) show that COP preserves utility across sequential requests without weakening forgetting, preventing the collapse of standard objectives and forgetting more effectively than continual-specific baselines.

Title: Numerical Integration on Bounded Domains via Learned Reflected Diffusions

  • Authors: Kirill Korolev, Artur Goldman, Timofei Gritsaev, Tigran Ramazyan, Nikita Morozov
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40499
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40499
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40499]] Numerical Integration on Bounded Domains via Learned Reflected Diffusions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40499)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: We study high-dimensional integration on bounded convex domains. Given a non-negative integrand $r$, the goal is to estimate $Z=\int_{D}r(x)\,\mathrm dx$ on $D\subset\mathbb R^d$. Standard Quasi-Monte Carlo does not adapt its evaluation points to the integrand, while effective importance sampling requires a proposal that captures its concentration. Learned diffusion samplers can supply such a proposal, but existing methods are formulated on $\mathbb R^d$ and do not respect the domain constraint $x\in D$. We instead build the proposal from reflected dynamics that stay in $D$ by construction. A reflected diffusion progressively transforms the target distribution toward the uniform distribution on $D$, and a neural score model is trained to reverse this process. A change-of-measure formula for reflected diffusions yields a training objective without additional boundary terms and an unbiased importance-sampling estimator of $Z$. For non-smooth integrands, where reparameterization gradients may be undefined or uninformative, we derive a REINFORCE estimator of the training gradient using Gaussian proposal densities before reflection, without evaluating reflected transition densities or differentiating $r$. We evaluate on numerous synthetic targets and real-world problems, demonstrating strong performance of the proposed method, as well as favorable scaling with the dimension of the problem and the number of points used for the Monte Carlo estimate.

  • Abstract: RecHash is a stateful sequential authentication construction based on paired hash-chain openings and an authenticated record history. It develops the decreasing position relation of the 2019 hash-entangled construction, whose exact integer normalization yields a two-coordinate constant-sum antichain. Direct proportional reuse is constrained by integer capacity and multiplicative contraction. The main construction instead assigns each record to a predetermined chain segment, with complementary positions encoding each digest block. All digests fit every scheduled slot, independently of preceding messages. For an $\ell$-bit digest distributed over $k$ equal-length chain pairs, balanced allocation gives $\lfloor L/(2^{\lceil\ell/k\rceil}+1)\rfloor$ slots. A 256-bit digest, 32 pairs, and length 65535 provide 254 application records followed by a renewal certificate, with 2048 bytes of openings per record for 256-bit chain outputs. We give a complete segmented state transition, a worked trace, exact allocation and resource results, and a renewal-free security-composition theorem conditional on authenticated history and an adaptive one-step guarantee. The analysis relates the construction to constant-sum encodings and standardized hash-based signatures; a concrete post-quantum reduction and security across renewal remain open.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40511

  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as computational models of human language processing, yet it remains unclear which components of their representations drive alignment with neural activity. Across three naturalistic language-listening datasets spanning intracranial electrophysiology and fMRI, and six model conditions spanning autoregressive, bidirectional masked, and randomly initialized language models, we find that full linear encoding performance can be recovered using only 1-3% of the original embedding dimensionality. Title: PhysCount: Do MLLMs Count Visual Appearances or Physical Entities?

  • Authors: Lidong Lu, Jiaxin Lv, Liangrui Dong, Xinyi Mao, Lidong Zou, Zhenquan Li, Tong Lu, Guo Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Images reveal appearances, but counting often asks about physical objects. Existing visual counting benchmarks rarely separate these two operations. We introduce PhysCount, a benchmark for evaluating whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can distinguish what appears in an image from what physically exists in the scene. PhysCount contains 546 real-world images and 4,595 human-written questions organized into 651 target-specific groups. For each group, annotators locate the relevant appearances, label how each is observed, and identify appearances of the same entity. Higher image resolution improves access to visual evidence but brings limited gains in complete-group accuracy, whereas answering related questions jointly produces a marked improvement. These results expose a gap between recognizing what is visible and correctly recovering the physical entities behind those observations.

Title: Power Adjusting Games Under Block-Dependent Pool Payout Schemes

  • Authors: Mustafa Doger, Sennur Ulukus
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.DC, cs.IT, math.PR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40558
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40558
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40558]] Power Adjusting Games Under Block-Dependent Pool Payout Schemes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40558)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Advanced power adjusting block withholding attacks such as PAW and T-PAW have been analyzed so far under a global-reset version of the proportional scheme (GRP), which is rarely used in practice. In this paper, we analyze T-PAW, which contains FAW and PAW as special cases, under the block-dependent payout schemes deployed in practice: Pay-Per-Last-N-Shares (PPLNS), round-based Proportional (PROP) and exponential-decay (EXP). PROP is highly vulnerable to PAH, while EXP faces a trade-off: a slow decay invites PAH and a fast decay invites T-PAW. PPLNS is the least vulnerable scheme: it is immune to PAH and it mitigates, but does not eliminate, T-PAW. All analytical results are verified by Monte Carlo simulations.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: On-policy Self-distillation (OPSD) has proven effective for post-training large language models by constructing a teacher conditioned on privileged references. In this paper, we argue that neither of them is sufficient. Instead, every token needs its own teacher, as the teacher preference can vary token by token within the same response. Experiments with Qwen3 models on mathematical reasoning tasks show that MTA-OPSD consistently outperforms existing OPSD approaches with an average gain of up to $+5.9$ over the base model, highlighting the importance of adapting teacher supervision at the token level.   • Keywords: watermark, large language model
  • Abstract: We present TextSeal2, a distortion-free watermark for large language models that keeps its signal under speculative decoding: tokens that replace rejected drafts remain watermarked with the same keys, and the acceptance rate is unchanged. It also preserves output diversity and adds no measurable inference latency. Its predecessor, TextSeal, restores diversity in the Gumbel-max scheme by routing each token to one of two keys via a coin flip. This is suboptimal because the detector averages the scores from both keys without knowing which key was used. TextSeal2 instead merges two or more keys into a single virtual key and couples it to fresh noise through AutoMark's Gaussian copula. Any number of keys, including publicly shared keys, can be merged this way without diluting detection. Under speculative decoding, TextSeal2 verifies keyed drafts with one more exponential race, run on the leftovers of the draft races. By memorylessness, this race reproduces block verification, so the output follows the target model exactly. We evaluate TextSeal2 on three LLMs. At equal output token diversity, TextSeal2 detects 8 to 22 percentage points more 100-token answers than TextSeal at a false-positive rate of $10^{-3}$, and is comparable to textGrain while requiring no post-logits optimization. Under speculative decoding, it detects 18 and 22 percentage points more 200-token answers than TextSeal and textGrain, respectively, at the same acceptance rate.

Title: Difficulty Calibration Across Time for Membership Inference in Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Michael Joseph, Yedid Hoshen
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40616
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40616
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40616]] Difficulty Calibration Across Time for Membership Inference in Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40616)
  • Keywords: attack, membership infer, diffusion
  • Abstract: Modern diffusion models are trained on massive datasets that often include copyrighted or private images. Membership inference attacks (MIAs) address this by testing whether a given image was in a model's training set. The standard approach scores an image by its reconstruction loss and flags low-loss images as members. This often fails because some images are simply easier to reconstruct than others, so a simple unseen image can score a lower loss than a complex training image. Difficulty calibration separates memorization from how difficult an image is to begin with. We show how to calibrate along two axes of time: training time and diffusion time. First, across training time, we introduce Reference-Model Calibration (RMC), which compares a fine-tuned model's loss against an earlier checkpoint. This cleanly isolates newly memorized data and gives near-perfect auditing, even without text captions. Since earlier checkpoints are not always available, we next present Temporal Self-Calibration (TSC), which calibrates across diffusion time using only a single model. Our key observation is that an image's difficulty persists across noise levels, whereas memorization is localized in time. By comparing the loss at a target timestep against neighboring timesteps from the same model, TSC attenuates image difficulty using the model's own trajectory. Empirically, our methods beat the state of the art by a wide margin, and unlike most existing attacks, generalize across different model parameterizations such as $x$-, $v$-, and $\epsilon$-prediction models. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: An ODE-based Sampler for Discrete Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Samson Gourevitch, Yazid Janati, Eric Moulines, Eric P. Xing, Alain Durmus, Giacomo Zanella
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40618
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40618
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40618]] An ODE-based Sampler for Discrete Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40618)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The marginals of a continuous diffusion model can be reproduced by a deterministic ordinary differential equation (ODE), known as the probability flow ODE, which provides a foundation for accelerated sampling and consistency distillation. In this paper we address the question whether an analogous formulation exists for discrete diffusion models and answer it affirmatively by introducing exponential residual flows (ERFs). Our framework augments the discrete state with a continuous residual variable for each token and position. These residuals evolve deterministically according to piecewise-defined ODEs whose vector fields depend on the current discrete state and the forward and backward rate matrices of the prescribed diffusion model. The discrete state changes whenever a residual reaches zero. We prove that this construction preserves the marginals of the original diffusion. We derive a practical discretization of our sampler and specialize it to masked and uniform diffusion. The deterministic trajectories of ERFs support consistency distillation and yield empirical improvements over previous approaches across a variety of benchmarks including code and text generation tasks.

Title: ShallowDefend: Harnessing Shallow Safety Alignment for Multi-Turn Defender

  • Authors: Qiyao Ma, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Mingqian Feng, Chengquan Guo, Jialin Song, Carolyn Buractaon, Zhe Zhao, Junshan Zhang, Sandeep Atluri, Christopher White, Jianfeng Gao
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40627
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40627
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40627]] ShallowDefend: Harnessing Shallow Safety Alignment for Multi-Turn Defender(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40627)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Safety alignment has enabled large language models to reliably refuse harmful requests directly, yet the same request distributed across a multi-turn conversation still succeeds far too often. This brittleness has a mechanistic root: Alignment is shallow, with safety behavior concentrated in a model's first few output tokens, and existing defenses inherit the weakness, supervising the surface form of the response without ever giving the model an explicit safety decision to learn. We take the opposite view of shallowness: if a short span determines the trajectory of an entire response, that span is the highest-leverage place to intervene, and the defender can occupy it first. We propose Assess $\to$ Decide $\to$ Respond, a framework in which the defender assesses the user's intent, commits to an explicit, parseable safety decision, and only then generates a reply conditioned on it, with the commitment trained by reinforcement learning at the payload turn. Because the decision is a token span, the reward can be a rule: a self-verified reward checks the committed label against the conversation's known nature, requiring no judge and no annotation, while the same format also supports an LLM-as-judge reward over a graded decision space with safe redirection. Across two model families and fourteen benchmarks spanning safety, utility, and helpfulness, our defenders achieve the strongest multi-turn safety in a controlled comparison, maintaining their lead in safety while preserving utility at a modest cost to helpfulness. A per-token KL analysis and interventional probes confirm why this works: training concentrates its effect on the brief deliberation that sets a response's trajectory, and the decision committed there is a causal control point. The very shallowness attackers exploit becomes the mechanism of defense.

Title: world2vec: Mechanistic world models through latent dynamics learning

  • Authors: Tobias Schmidt, Rodrigo Gonzalez Laiz, Krisztián Szuppinger, Steffen Schneider
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40637
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40637
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40637]] world2vec: Mechanistic world models through latent dynamics learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40637)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: World models can achieve strong control performance without faithfully capturing the dynamics of their environment, limiting their potential for mechanistic interpretability and scientific discovery. Here, we introduce world2vec to learn mechanistic world models without reward information directly from observations and actions. World2vec learns latent dynamics that faithfully reproduce the true system over long prediction horizons, with at least one order of magnitude fewer parameters than DreamerV3, TD-MPC2 and LeWM. This enables state of the art performance in controlling continuous environments from pixels and accurate system identification across a benchmark of 71 attractors, demonstrating how world models are amenable to scientific discovery.

Title: SonoWorld4D: From One Video to a 4D Audio-Visual Scene via Diffusion Synchronization

  • Authors: Derong Jin, Xiyi Chen, Ming C. Lin, Ruohan Gao
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.SD
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40640
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40640
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40640]] SonoWorld4D: From One Video to a 4D Audio-Visual Scene via Diffusion Synchronization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40640)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Recent progress in visual scene generation has extended beyond static 3D scenes toward the more challenging problem of generating dynamic 4D scenes from minimal input. However, sound, despite being intrinsically coupled with dynamic objects and events, remains largely unmodeled in prior work. We introduce Video-to-4DAVScene, a new task that turns a video into an explorable, time-varying 4D audio-visual scene, and present the SonoWorld4D framework for this task. Given a monocular video, our method generates a 360 static background, reconstructs explicit motion trajectories of dynamic, sound-emitting foreground objects, and synthesizes a physically-grounded sound field consistent across multiple sources through dual-space audio diffusion synchronization. The resulting 4D scene supports real-time novel-view visual and spatial audio rendering. Evaluation on real-world dynamic scenes and a user study demonstrates SonoWorld4D generates 4D audio-visual scenes with perceptually realistic, physically grounded spatial audio consistent with scene geometry and visual content.

Title: Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application

  • Authors: Quentin Dhôte, Romaric Audigier
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40643
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40643
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40643]] Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40643)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Luggage re-identification (ReID) is a critical task in video surveillance for forensics, security or border management (suspicious or abandoned luggage recognition), as well as luggage management (lost luggage recovery). It enables the association of observations of the same luggage across disjoint camera views in complex environments such as airports, train stations and border crossing. While existing approaches primarily focus on appearance-based cropped image matching under relatively clean conditions, real-world scenarios are characterized by frequent occlusions, cluttered background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify luggage. We evaluate different variants on a benchmark dataset acquired in real-world environments to address the questions of selecting and merging different views and shots of the same luggage as well as training on different environment datasets for an overall ReID performance improvement in practical applications.

Title: Read, Allocate, Route: Role-Coupled Tokenization for Pixel-Space Generative Transformers

  • Authors: Jiongfan Yang, Weichen Fan, Haiwen Diao, Zhongang Cai, Ziwei Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.NE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40647
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40647
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40647]] Read, Allocate, Route: Role-Coupled Tokenization for Pixel-Space Generative Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40647)
  • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Pixel-space generative Transformers typically use one token per image patch, so capturing finer detail requires more tokens and makes global attention increasingly expensive. We observe that finer detail does not necessarily require more global communication: low-frequency structure benefits from global exchange, while high-frequency detail can be modeled locally. Based on this insight, we introduce SpecSlot, a pixel-space rectified-flow Transformer that decouples output detail from the length of the global attention sequence. SpecSlot expands each spatial anchor into multiple slots, each assigned a disjoint band of DCT coefficients; this separates anchor density from spectral subdivision. Only the lowest-frequency slot from each anchor participates in global attention, while all slots interact locally. This allows the model to increase spectral detail without enlarging the global sequence. A spatial head further adds a local correction to the spectral prediction, while the generative state, target, and loss remain in pixel space. On ImageNet-256, SpecSlot achieves a comparable final FID to a FLOPs-matched JiT control while converging faster. On ImageNet-512, it improves FID by 2.84 over the control with 3.9% fewer FLOPs, and comes within only 0.69 FID of JiT-B/16 at 38.2% lower cost. On text-to-image generation, SpecSlot surpasses JiT-B/8 on DPG-Bench by 1.06 at 40.6% lower forward cost.

Title: Propose-Filter-Release: Scaling Noise to Typical Sensitivity via Private Filtering

  • Authors: Moshe Shenfeld, Eliad Tsfadia, Katrina Ligett
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40654
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40654
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.40654]] Propose-Filter-Release: Scaling Noise to Typical Sensitivity via Private Filtering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40654)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect
  • Abstract: An estimator can be accurate and stable on typical data, yet sensitive to a few possible records. Differentially private mechanisms commonly scale their noise to the worst-case influence of a single record, which lets these records dictate the noise even when the remaining data would allow far less. Removing them first would restore the smaller scale, but the removal must itself be private. We introduce \emph{propose-filter-release} (PFR), which, by privately filtering its input, turns a mechanism that protects the addition or removal of a typical record, one that fits the remaining data, into one that is differentially private in the standard sense, on every input. Using our novel filtering step, PFR requires only a concave friendliness score, which measures how well a record fits a dataset, and a release mechanism that is friendly-DP, that is, private when the changed record fits the remaining data. For every such score and release mechanism, it guarantees differential privacy under public conditions on the number of records, so it is a general privacy primitive, comparable to propose-test-release, and FriendlyCore can be viewed as one of its implementations. We illustrate the generality of PFR by instantiating it for scalar statistics, principal subspace estimation, the KL divergence of user histograms, the effective resistances of a graph, and treatment-rule selection.
